You should, however, take it with a hundredth part of common water or milk after the stomach is well filled, for the reason that the internal nature <note>The "internal nature" refers to the digestive heat or the vital spirit within the body</note> then opens itself up and dissolves. Through the power of this medicine, nature can be preserved for one hundred and fifty years.

This practice of Aristotle—as Arnold the Greek <note>Likely referring to Arnaldus de Villa Nova, a famous medieval alchemist and physician</note> says in the preface to the *Book of the Apple* <note>original: "Buch vom Apfel"; a pseudo-Aristotelian work titled *Liber de Pomo*</note>, and according to an interpreter of Holy Scripture—has a power and virtue equal to the water in Paradise, which by its very nature makes a person immortal, healthy, fresh, joyful, and everlasting.

From this water, all true philosophers have derived their lives and countless riches. Therefore, it is better to study wisdom than to become wealthy, for the former encompasses within itself wealth and all bodily and earthly happiness.

For this reason, this blessed stone is also called <term>Rebis</term> <note>A Latin alchemical term meaning "two-thing," representing the union of opposites like male and female or spirit and body</note>, because it contains within itself the perfection of all things. It is also called an "Egg" because of its roundness, as it is spherical, or because it possesses the form and shape of the Great World <note>The Macrocosm or the Universe</note>, which has a round shape and encloses and encompasses all other things within itself.

See how clearly I have shown you the truth of the writing. Yet you should know that this work is divided into five parts: namely, the conception of the materials—which must be of a healthy, right, and well-disposed nature, of moderate stature, and

of the air, since the external vapors sometimes cool the vessel at the top, and are a cause of the transformation of that which is within.

The **manure heap** <note>A "manure heap" or *fimus equinus* was a standard alchemical method for providing a gentle, consistent, "natural" heat for digestion.</note>, however, should be made between two strong walls, in a circle, because the warmth is better retained there, and the place should be inspected often, for the sake of avoiding unnatural scorching, so that neither the wind nor external air can harm it.

And here, great care must be taken regarding the <term>distillation</term> <note>The process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and condensing it back into liquid.</note> of the aforementioned materials, so that the fire is always kept in a steady, uniform governance, just as in the <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The process of breaking down a substance through gentle heat, often the first step in the "Great Work."</note>. Certainly, it must be distilled in such a way that the oil does not show itself during the distillation; for the oil is only caused by the change brought about by the improper decoction of the materials. Therefore, where the oil appears, there is no hope of a perfect distillation, but rather of final ruin.

But there is a remedy in this matter: that when the <term>oil</term> <note>original: "Oleum"</note> begins to distill, one should remove the <term>alembic</term> <note>The upper part of a still, used for cooling and directing vapors.</note> and place it, in the aforementioned order, back into putrefaction with the water that has been drawn out of the material. Then, such weakness will be remedied with a moderate fire. This happens, however, in a somewhat longer time, because one must maintain this putrefaction for eight days continuously, and then it shall be distilled once more, as indicated above. Therefore, in the event of the failure of one method, one must immediately know the counsel for the other.

It is, however, to be held as a general rule—

->❦ ) o ( ❦<-

...that in this work, never more than two elements are seen, namely Earth and Water. For within the Earth, Fire is contained, and within the Water, Air. Therefore, *Aristotle* says in the secrets to *Alexander*: <note>original: "Mache die Erde vollkommen mit ihrem himmlischen Wasser..." referring to the pseudo-Aristotelian Secretum Secretorum</note> Make the Earth perfect with its heavenly Water, and your whole work will become perfect.

This same Earth is an Earth of the fifth essence <term>Quinta Essentia: the "fifth element" or purest spiritual essence of a substance</term>, for it is brought to such a degree, insofar as the fifth essence is the highest effect of nature and power in every thing; for by its own nature, it cannot be brought to any other external degree. And in such a degree is all the power and property that one can possibly have in a body under the sun, for its nature ceases to stand in the fourth degree, but it takes on the fifth from the elements from without.

But now note this one great secret: that every *Fifth Essence* is in itself a preserver of the health of every body. And it has within itself a power and effect in every thing that is of that very same *complexion* <term>Complexion: the specific balance of humors or qualities in a substance</term> and essence, and not of another.

Just as the *Fifth Essence* of poison works only toward health in those things that are poisonous, so it is with others. Thus, the *Fifth Essence* of metals cures all weaknesses of metals. From this, one can now conclude that the science of the Stone of the Wise <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> is truthful, infallible, and without falsehood.

If science seeks to cure the weaknesses of metals, and does not do so through their <term>fifth essence</term> <note>original: "fünftes Wesen" or "Quintessence," the purest and most potent form of a substance</note>, then it labors in vain; its search is useless and finds neither end, nor salvation, nor health.

Therefore, when the Earth is artificially brought to the <term>fifth essence</term>, and the Water is brought to the same form, then perfection arises from the two united together. Through this, all *philosophers* <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval alchemists</note> have possessed that which was necessary for the health of the body and the maintenance of life.

Hence *Messahalach* <note>likely Masha'allah ibn Athari, an 8th-century Persian-Jewish astrologer and alchemist</note> says in the *Book of the Perfect Masterpiece*:

> It is a vain and recklessly foolish thing to wish to bring something to the <term>fifth essence</term>—from which all *philosophers* have maintained their lives—and yet to destroy and spoil that same <term>fifth essence</term> before one reaches the end. For thus the foolish laborers work in powders and destroy the original properties, namely the natural radical moisture, <term>radical moisture</term> <note>original: "humidum radicale," the vital fluid believed to sustain life and matter</note> and the <term>nourishing moisture</term> <note>original: "Cibale," referring to the moisture that provides sustenance to the substance</note>, and they end in sadness, labor, and misery.

Let us now return to our manner of writing, namely to our <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, and how one should make it white. This can be done through the third part of its water, and no more, in each moistening under a heat and moderation of the properties.

But its **Water** should always be kept clean in a fine place in a stoppered glass vessel; also, the water should not be distilled more than once because of its weakness, lest it be transformed into a stone—namely, into a useless powder—through the loss of its power. Also, the aforementioned water should always be covered so that it does not evaporate or smoke away, and thus the **flower** <note>The "flower" refers to the most volatile and precious spirit of the substance</note> perish.

And this I have tested once before, finding that there had been an error in a work I had begun; therefore, I took a new water of the very same property, power, and virtue, and produced from it what I desired.

Now it remains to speak of the errors that occur in the work, through which many wander from the right path. One should therefore note that the first error lies in not maintaining the fire steadily and continuously, or else in using a contrary fire in the same work, as the aforementioned Greek <note>Likely a reference to Hermes Trismegistus or another foundational Greek alchemical authority</note> has said. For one must pay attention not only to the warmth, but also to the *quality* of the warmth. The fire should be warm and moist, as in a bath; it should also be dry, so that it may perform different functions in different things. For one type of fire is useful for a perfect dissolution <term>dissolution: the process of breaking a solid down into liquid form</term>, such as horse manure <note>Horse manure was frequently used in alchemy as a source of steady, gentle "digestive" heat as it fermented</note>. But the other fire brings about a different dissolution and creates a change in the *matter* <note>original: "Materia"</note>. Therefore, the true *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophi"; referring to alchemists</note> say that in the perfect work there should be only one perfect fire, one governance, one vessel, and thus in all things there will be an equality and harmony of the entire work.

This same fire, however, must be kept moderate in the labor from degree to degree, so that until the "whiteness" <note>The *albedo*, or the second major stage of the alchemical Great Work, signifying purification</note> it is always quite gentle, like the natural warmth in a human being, or like the warmth of the eggs which the hen in her

In the first labor and in the <term>putrefaction</term><note>The process of breaking down a substance through rot or decay to release its essence.</note>, its true essence passes into its <term>Fifth Essence</term><note>original: "Quintam Essentiam"; the purest, most concentrated form of a substance.</note>, which is the beginning of our perfect work and the masterpiece. If any of these are lacking, it will have no successful effect, unless one works more cautiously and subtly.

The second error lies in the Matter from which the Stone is brought into its beginning and effect. The third error is in the vessel: For the vessel should be round, and above all things round at the bottom, with properly shaped sides, and with an upper part that is almost twice as large as its bottom, and it should be almost one cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or the length of a forearm.</note> high. Likewise, it should be made of glass, not porous, and well-sealed with a suitable lute <note>A specialized alchemical cement or clay used to hermetically seal vessels.</note>. But this should happen in the beginning after the material <note>original: "Materia"</note> has smoked off in its perfect selection after its perfect birth, as is mentioned above.

One must also take care that the evaporation of the material does not last longer than its heat remains, which sometimes tends to last for about 3 hours. From this we can learn that in this work one must do nothing else than let the vessel with the material stand in a separate place covered with a cloth, so that no dust falls into it, and the accidental moisture of the cloth is entirely driven out from the vessel. And this must happen for two reasons: Firstly, because of the rapid change of nature; secondly, for the expulsion of accidental moistures, which might cause the living material in our Stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary goal of alchemy.</note> to be destroyed, and prevent it from reaching the ash-gray color.

The fourth error occurs at times in the distillation, namely when the material is distilled more than is proper, or too much into the

Then, in the water, there are specifically three things: the spirit, the soul, and the <term>tincture<note>In alchemy, a tincture is a potent liquid extract capable of transforming or healing substances.</note></term>. However, the spirit and the soul cannot enter except through a sharp and corrosive power; although, through various <term>distillations<note>The process of purifying a liquid by heating and cooling.</note></term>, the power and nature are certainly weakened. And if the water should be cloudy and not clear, it rectifies itself without distillation, and whatever remains at the bottom should be thrown away.

The fifth error lies in the <term>rectification<note>The process of refining or purifying a substance, often through repeated distillation or filtration.</note></term> of the earth, and this should happen gradually and by degrees, such that as often as it is moistened, it should also be made dry again. Therefore, there are three parts of water to one part of its earth—and sometimes more, if it requires it—for the sake of perfect dissolution. And the perfect <term>dissolution<note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note></term> in this work is its most perfect <term>Quinta Essentia<note>The "Fifth Essence" or Quintessence; the purest and most concentrated medicinal or spiritual force.</note></term>, which contains within itself all medicinal power and a wonderfully beautiful scent, full of all sweetness.

There are three signs of a perfect dissolution. First, that it is truly water in itself without impurity; second, that it is white as milk on the surface; third, that it is as sweet as honey to the taste on the tongue. **NB.** <note>Latin: Nota Bene, meaning "Note Well."</note> This aforementioned <term>matter<note>original: "Materia"</note></term>, however, becomes hard of its own accord with warm and dry fire, and dissolves itself in warm and moist fire. it is then also called by the names of all stones, such as mercury, sulfur, orpiment <note>a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, and it is called by many other names, and yet none of these are present in its physical nature, but rather in its power and virtue. For it is a mercury because it possesses within itself a perfect power and life. It is also the incombustible sulfur, and therefore it carries all signs with it according to the primary meaning: For a sign does not take its name from its final meaning, but from its most noble meaning.

The sixth and last error consists in the constant maintenance of the fire and the application of the tincture. For if an interruption should occur in the heat, or a breach in the vessel or in the timing, the incineration of the work would follow. For the time is uniform and of one kind, namely: in the <term>putrefaction</term> <note>the stage of breaking down or "rotting" the matter</note> 40 days; for the white stage, 40 days; and for the red stage, 40 days. For the perfect dissolution, 40 days, provided that the governance of the fire has been employed in the proper manner.

In the first period it is called a stone and yet not a stone; in the second, a stone; in the third, <term>living silver</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; commonly known as quicksilver or mercury</note>; in the fourth, a ruby. These names are given to it by Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, and in part by Aristotle and Morienus <note>a 7th-century hermit and famous alchemist</note>.

The sign in the <term>putrefaction</term> is thinness accompanied by the deepest blackness. The sign of the <term>distillation</term> or <term>calcination</term> <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to effect change</note> of the stone is a saturation with an ash-gray color. The sign of the whiteness is that it shines like white

Lime without any mixed blackness. The sign of the dissolution is that nothing remains undissolved, but rather that it becomes entirely like a standing clear water, without impurity. The application <note>original: "Auftragung"; the process of projecting the alchemical medicine onto base metals</note> of our Stone, however, is that first one part of the mentioned blessed Stone, which has been hardened in warm ashes, shall be applied to 10 parts of common mercury <note>original: "Mercurji"</note> which has been made warm, so that the said mercury is thus incorporated with the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapide"; Latin for stone</note> and made fixed, and then cast upon 100 parts. Indeed, let it be cast upon whatever <term>Metals</term> <note>original: "Metalla"; Latin plural for metals</note> one wishes, be they as imperfect as they may, it will nonetheless tincture them according to the property of the medicine, namely white or red, so that no mineral or metal can be found more perfect, unless it be for curing the weaknesses of the body. For the gold from the ore-mines has essentially something divine within itself in its first creation and in its first work.

## Book of Wisdom. Chapter VII. verses 8, 9, 11.

I esteemed Wisdom more highly than kingdoms and principalities, and I considered wealth as nothing compared to her. I did not liken any precious stone to her, for all gold is like meager sand in her presence, and silver is to be accounted as mud <note>original: "Koth"; refers to filth or common mire</note> compared to her. But all good things came to me along with her, and countless riches in her hand.

# The Beginning of the Rosary,

## which the most secret Mystery of the Sages is contained,

### Described by John Dastin <note>original: "Johanne Daustenio," a 14th-century English alchemist known for his correspondence with Pope John XXII</note>, an Englishman and well-experienced Philosopher.

First, it is to be known that this delightful and very precious work has been composed from the books of the ancients. We intend, however, to summarize everything briefly here, so that the most excellent as well as the most certain mystery may be sufficiently revealed to you, dear reader. We call this short treatise a "Rose Garden" <note>original: "Rosen-Garten" or "Rosarium," a common title for medieval alchemical compendiums</note> for this reason: because we have plucked and gathered it for your benefit from the books of the ancients, like roses from the thorn-hedges. And because everything that runs contrary to truth or good reason is harmful to the truth, we intend to teach briefly—using sayings that agree entirely with the truth, in clear speech and proper order, word for word, with sufficient reasons attached—everything we have found in their books that is necessary for the completion of the Work <note>The "Great Work" or "Magnum Opus" of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and

## CHAPTER I.

All works that the benevolent God has created move, as it were, in a circle and are perfect; they return back to the place from whence they came, driven around in a round. For in the beginning of Creation, when God the Lord established Nature as the beginning of all things, He made four simple bodies <note>The four classical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth</note>, from which He afterwards composed all mixed bodies.

Among the mixed bodies, however, He made some that possess reason, some that possess sensitivity or senses, others that grow out of the earth, and others that originate within the earth.

And indeed, all those who possess reason He created from the most subtle part of the elements in His own image. Therefore, our heart is also not at rest until it returns to Him again. For the subtle part of all elements ascends up to the fire that is among the stars: since we are created from such a part, we should rightly long for God as our first origin.

But everything that has sensitivity, and all things that grow out of the earth, and those in

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural philosophy and the composition of matter, specifically focusing on the distinction between organic life and metals.</meta>

) o (

Those things which arise from the earth are composed of the coarser parts of the elements and are divided into various kinds. When these are dissolved and separated by death, they rightly return again to earth and water as their primary matter <note>original: "materien"</note>. For the coarse and thick part of all elements naturally descends to its center point—that is, to the earth, as the mother of all earthly things.

However, all natural things also have a certain goal of size and increase, so that each may multiply its own kind within its species. Some of these are composed of unequal parts, such as flesh, blood, bone, veins, nerves, and the like, as is the case with man; or of wood, bark, leaves, and the like, as is a tree. Others, however, are composed of equal parts, being things that are of one essence throughout, as can be seen in the metals.

Those which consist of unequal pieces have their own seed <note>original: "Saamen"</note> within them, from which they are multiplied and increased, as is seen in all living creatures and in plants.

But those which consist of equal pieces are not multiplied unless they are first brought back into their primary matter <note>original: "materiam"</note>. Therefore the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Aristotle or a high-ranking alchemical authority like Hermes Trismegistus</note> says: The masters of alchemy <note>original: "Alchimiæ"</note> should know that the species of metals cannot truly and properly be transformed unless they are perhaps reduced into their primary mat-

brought back to their original state, and then they are transformed and turned into something different than they were before. And this occurs because the decay of one thing is the birth of another, in artificial as well as in natural things. For Art imitates Nature, and in some respects, it improves and surpasses it, just as a weak constitution is changed through the physicians' art and diligence. Nature builds no house, nor does she make an <term>Electuary</term> <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</note> or anything of the kind, since she has no movement of her own to perform such a task. So it is also with our <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical Great Work</note>; although it possesses the <term>Tincture</term> <note>The spiritual or medicinal essence capable of transforming base metals into gold</note> within itself by nature (for it is created perfectly within the earth), it nevertheless has no movement of its own to become a perfect <term>Elixir</term> unless it is driven and promoted toward that end by Art.

Therefore, Art occasionally produces something which Nature cannot accomplish on her own. Art, however, follows Nature and completes natural things to the extent that they are naturally inclined to become perfect; and for that reason, one must come to the aid of Nature through Art at the point where Nature has ceased.

There is also no difference between Nature and Art, except only that Art works from the outside, while Nature works from within. For Art, acting as a tool, gives movement to Nature, but Nature works of herself because she strives to reach her <term>Perfection</term>.

Furthermore, every body is either an element or composed of elements: But the generation of every composite body consists of the four simple elements. Therefore, it is necessary that our stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the goal of the alchemical Great Work.</note> be brought back to the first origin of its sulfur, and the mercury be divided into the elements; otherwise, it can neither be purified nor put back together, because its most subtle and smallest parts cannot enter into the smallest parts of the body. But when it is divided, it is purified and composed, and creates an elixir that we seek and desire. For the trial of the fire destroys its own particular form and introduces a new shape.

After the separation of the elements, however, one can see or grasp nothing of them except for the earth and the water. Because one can nowhere see air and fire, nor perceive their power, except only within the former two elements: Since they are far too thin, subtle, and simple, they cannot be beheld by bodily eyes at all.

Therefore, you should not trouble yourself greatly concerning them, since it is enough that you simply bring the stone to purity.

## CHAPTER II.

There are, however, four elements and four humors <note>The four bodily fluids that were believed to govern human health and temperament.</note> such as blood, choleric moisture <note>Yellow bile.</note>, phlegmatic moisture <note>Phlegm.</note>, and black bile: And

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural philosophy and the fundamental building blocks of the physical world, specifically the relationship between the four qualities and the four elements.</meta>

# 110 ( Cancer ) 0 ( Capricorn )

There are four qualities: Moist, warm, cold, and dry. The elements <term>Elementa; the fundamental substances of the universe</term> are these: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth. Of these, two are inclined toward one another, and two are opposed to one another. Two are active <note>original: "würkende"</note>, and two are passive <note>original: "leidende"</note>.

Fire and Air can get along well with each other, as can Water and Earth. Fire and Water are opposed to each other, as are Air and Earth.

Fire and Air are active; Water and Earth are passive. Two rise into the heights, and two move downward. One is in the middle between the other two, and this is for the reason that whenever an opposing thing is far removed from its opposite, they cannot be reconciled except through a medium <note>the "middle" element that shares qualities with both extremes</note>.

Heat is opposed to cold, and the moist to the dry. From this it is evident that none of these can be united with the other except through the medium.

Therefore, whatever is not opposing is united in itself. Just as the warm part is united with the dry part—because they are in no way contrary to one another—the element of Fire is composed from them.

Likewise, the warm and the moist are not opposed to each other; therefore, the element of Air arises from them. The cold and the dry are also not opposed to each other, and from these the element of Earth arises. Just as also the cold...

But Heat and Cold cannot be united, except solely through a medium—namely, through Dryness and Moisture—because otherwise they cannot tolerate one another on their own, inasmuch as one stifles the other due to its contrary property. However, through the Moist and the Dry, they can indeed exist together, because one sustains the other.

Furthermore, Heat and Cold press together, and also separate those things which are of a like nature (and not dissimilar), through <term>Dissolving</term><note>original: Dissolviren; the process of breaking a solid down into liquid</note> and <term>Coagulating</term><note>original: Coaguliren; the process of thickening or solidifying a liquid</note>. But Moisture and Dryness are bound and also separated through contraction and moistening.

From this, it follows that a simple birth, as well as a natural transformation, is an effect of the elements. From this it is evident that all changeable things are simply generated and naturally transformed through Heat and Cold.

Yet, every thing does not come from just any thing; rather, from a specific thing comes something specific, for there is no true birth except solely from such things that agree in their nature.

## CHAPTER III.

However, it is called into doubt what that substance might be of which the <term>Philosophers</term><note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval alchemists</note> speak, from which the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term><note>The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life</note> is prepared,

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the confusion among various authors regarding the "First Matter" or the starting material of the Great Work. This page continues the argument for the unity and purity of that substance.</meta>

) 0 (

because none among them expressly names the same; for one has posited this, and another has posited that, even though the **Truth** <note>original: "Warheit"</note> of it consists in one single thing alone. This very thing we truly guard most highly, and we teach to avoid all others. For it is known from the **Writings of the Sages** <note>A common alchemical reference to the collective wisdom of historical alchemists like Hermes Trismegistus or Geber.</note> that it is but one thing, and nothing foreign shall be added to it; for nothing is suited to the thing except that which is most closely related to it and of its own nature.

For natural things can bring forth nothing except what is like themselves, and they produce no other fruits than their own. Just as a human begets a human; a lion, a lion; and thus every creature begets its own kind. The mule, because it is of a different species <note>original: "unterschiedenem Geschlecht"</note>, never conceives nor bears an offspring like its parents.

So it is with all things born of a different kind. Accordingly, when things are of the same kind, their roots are also the same. And thus the **multiplicity** of things is caused by the difference of their parts. So learn now to distinguish the pure from the impure; for what a thing does not possess, it cannot give. The **Pure** is of only one essence without change; the **Impure** is manifold and composed of contrary parts, and therefore it can easily perish, as is evident in **Gold** and in **Lead**; for **Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for Lead, associated with the planet Saturn.</note>, that is

Lead can be completely brought to nothing; Gold, however, as the Sun, cannot be destroyed at all, and therefore all planets require its light.

Therefore, the knowledge of the opposition of the bodies, and the beginning of their creation, makes for an easy approach to this masterpiece <note>The "masterpiece" or "Magisterium" refers to the successful completion of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. For one impurity clings to another because they are of the same kind, but the pure overcomes the nature of the impure and allows no defect in the mixture. Therefore, you should not seek in Nature what is not within her. Since a thing cannot be generated except according to its own nature, there is also a greater corruption in the passive thing than in the active.

The tincture <term>Tincture: a potent alchemical liquid or essence capable of transforming metals</term> is in the Stone just as the heart is in man. Therefore, use the noblest and simplest member, and you will have enough. The part of the heart from the body is like the part of the brain from the head; for in the heart is the power of the irrational soul, just as in the brain is the power of the rational soul. Our beginning is humble and simple, not mixed, and therefore it is a simple body. So let go of the mixed and use the simple, for it is the first and the last among the planets, like the Sun among the stars.

Now I will turn my speech to the uniform things in Nature, although they are very strong. For in those things that have an equality, a transformation from one into another can easily occur. Therefore

Since your question concerns the nature of the two lights of the world <note>The Sun and the Moon, representing Gold and Silver in alchemical symbolism</note>, you must choose within them that which is uniform to them. And certainly, because this species is very properly suited to man, we know that the Work cannot be accomplished without him. Therefore, it is described by Aristotle to Alexander <note>Referring to the *Secretum Secretorum*, a popular medieval text falsely attributed to Aristotle</note> in this way: The crowns of kings are adorned with precious stones, by whose beauty the sight is strengthened, the spirit is delighted, and majesty is adorned; and through their power and virtue, highly burdensome diseases are turned away from bodies, without which all medicine can accomplish little. Therefore, physicians use them in their medicines to expel difficult and dangerous illnesses.

However, it is the true proof of the learned that the best stone among all stones is best suited to expel all weaknesses. <note>Note here concerning the best stone of all stones, which is to be understood as Gold.</note> For the best stone among all stones is that which is more "cooked" <term>digested or refined by heat</term>, and is closest to the fire, which can best endure the fire, and is more slowly broken by the fire. Therefore, noble stones are more useful—namely a ruby and sapphire—than other stones, because they are conceived in a warmer place, closer to the sun and amidst greater heat. And likewise among the metals, gold is more powerful than silver, for it is more cooked; and silver more so than copper or any other metal. And just as the

Just as the ruby contains within itself the effects of all other precious stones, so too does gold possess within itself the power of all metals that allow themselves to be extended <note>meaning malleable or ductile metals</note>. For it encompasses all metals, tinctures them, and makes them living, because it casts its light upwards as far as Saturn <note>the metal lead</note> and downwards as far as the Moon <note>the metal silver</note>; because it is nobler than all of them, you should therefore take gold.

But the ruby has in its complexion <note>its internal temperament or constitution</note> an equality of the elements, because it is for the most part of a bright-shining substance, as if from a clear water. And because it is hardened in a dry and great heat, it can neither be forged nor brought into another state, unless it be entirely destroyed, because it is resistant to fire in all respects.

Gold, however, because it is of an earthly substance mixed through and through with water, is uniform with fire and is hardened with cold after the heat has worked upon it. Therefore, it is beaten under the hammer, it is cast, and is improved in the fire. Because it has an equal mass in its good complexion, it is composed more uniformly, nobly, and durably than all other precious stones.

Immediately after this is silver; therefore, take also silver for the Stone <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>. In the other bodies, however, there is a deficiency, because in them there is either far too great a heat or too little cold. Just as cold is lacking in copper and iron, so heat is lacking in lead and tin. And their nature cannot become gold—

where they do not first become silver; the reason being that a thing cannot pass from the first to the third without going through the second. <note>The author refers to the alchemical progression of metals, where base metals must be "purified" into silver before reaching the perfection of gold.</note>

Thus, gold and silver are correctly and uniformly composed, and are properly tempered above all other stones <note>In this context, "stones" refers to minerals or metallic ores.</note> that exist under heaven, and they belong to the form of our most precious stone. And in truth, gold is a lord of stones, the noblest among bodies, the king and most distinguished head among them. For it is not corrupted by the air, nor by water, nor by the earth. Fire also takes nothing away from it; indeed, it becomes more moist through fire, becomes more beautiful and is improved. And burning things do not consume it, nor can corrupting things destroy it, because it is evenly mixed, and its nature is balanced in equal heat and cold, moisture and dryness. There is nothing superfluous in it, nor is anything lacking.

Therefore, there are no stones—even if they are valued more dearly than gold—that can be more powerful than gold. They are indeed more expensive, not because they ought to surpass the value of gold, but because they are rarer and less frequently found. Consequently, among all things known to man, gold is the best, for it is a ferment of the elixir <note>term: Ferment (a substance, like yeast, that transforms or "leavens" a larger mass into a higher state)</note>, without which the elixir can in no way be made. It gladdens the spirit, preserves youth, renews old age, and drives away all weakness of the body.

## CHAPTER IV.

The first matter of metals is mercury, for when they are melted, they are transformed into it. It is certain that every thing consists of that into which it is dissolved. Ice is dissolved into water by means of heat, from which it is then evident that it was previously water.

In the same way, all metals can be dissolved into mercury; therefore, they were previously mercury. You should now take note of this regarding the dissolution of bodies into mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"</note>.

So let God be praised, the Creator of excellent natures, who has given us the way and manner to destroy every thing and bring it back into its first nature and matter: namely, to bring the pure, prepared bodies back to the first origin of their <term>Sulfur</term> <note>The active, combustible principle in alchemy</note> and their <term>Mercury</term> <note>The passive, fluid principle in alchemy</note>, so that from them we can make above the earth in a few days the same thing that nature has made under the earth in a thousand years.

Therefore, even if we had nothing more good to expect from <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argento vivo"</note> than that it only makes the bodies subtle, as is its nature, we should still be satisfied with it. For it is dear and pleasant to the metals, and a medium which composes the <term>Tinctures</term> <note>The transformative essences or "Elixirs" sought by alchemists</note>. For it takes to itself what is of its nature, but rejects what is foreign, because it is of a uniform essence and of the same kind in all its parts.

It is Mercury <note>The author uses "Mercurius" to refer both to the planet/deity and the "philosophical mercury" or essence of the metal.</note> also that overcomes the fire, and is not overcome by it, but rather rests gracefully within it and rejoices in it. For because of the good cohesion of its parts in its strong mixture—namely, when its parts are made thick by the fire—it does not allow itself to be further corrupted. Nor, when a smoking flame is added to it, does it allow itself to be raised up into a smoke: since it does not allow itself to be killed <note>In alchemy, "killing" a metal often refers to calcination or the destruction of its metallic properties.</note> because of its hardness and incombustibility.

Therefore, we wish to indicate here with truth that those bodies have more perfection which have much mercury with them; but those which have little of it are not so perfect. And certainly, the inferiority of the imperfect bodies comes solely from the fact that they have little mercury and are not made fixed <term>Fixation: the process of turning a volatile substance into a solid that can resist fire</term>. Therefore, the perfection of the same is the increase of the mercury, a good hardening, and a lasting fixation.

Therefore, apply yourself, when you are at work, to overcome Mercury alone in its mixture; and if you can complete the work through it alone, then you will be a discoverer of the most precious perfection, and you will greatly rejoice over such complete perfection, which surpasses the work of nature. For it can be purified through and through, which nature cannot achieve: and thus, subsequently, such a work can be made from it that surpasses all natures: for there is no difference whether such a thing happens in natural in-

From this you may now grasp a secret: namely, that our <term>Medicine</term><note>In alchemy, the "Medicine" refers to the Elixir or Tincture capable of perfecting base metals or healing the body.</note> must necessarily be taken from those things which cling most closely to the internal nature of mercury, and are thoroughly mixed with it before it flies away <note>A reference to the volatility of mercury, which evaporates easily when heated.</note>.

One mercury, however, clings most to another and is closely befriended by it. After that comes gold, and then silver. Other bodies, however, do not have such great uniformity with it, because they have received less of its pure nature.

Furthermore, nothing is destroyed within it except for gold. Therefore, it is necessary that this Medicine must be extracted primarily from those things in which it most exists. It is present by nature both in the bodies <note>The "bodies" refer to the solid metals.</note> and in the mercury, because it is found that they possess the same properties. However, it is somewhat more difficult to extract from the bodies, and more accessible in the mercury, though not more perfect.

<margin>X</margin> For when it is raw or uncooked, it is called <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note>, a permanent water, lead, the <term>saliva of the Moon</term> <note>original: "Mondes Speichel"; a poetic term for lunar moisture or silver in its first stage</note>, tin, and the like. For such and similar names are given to it because of the excellence of its nature.

But let its names be as manifold as they will, it is nevertheless always only one single nature. To this you shall add nothing foreign, neither powder nor water, nor anything else. Your will must also be constant in the work, so that you do not undertake to try now this, now that; for our art does not consist in a multiplicity of things. For there is one <term>Stone</term>, one <term>Matter</term> <note>original: "Materia"</note>, one <term>Vessel</term>, one governance, and one arrangement, wherein we add nothing foreign, and also take nothing from it, except only that in the preparation we do away with the superfluous. <margin>X</margin> For nothing enters into it that has not come from it, neither into one of its parts, nor into the whole. But where anything foreign is added to it, it is immediately corrupted, and that which is sought and desired cannot come from it.

## CHAPTER V.

Thus our Stone is a single thing, namely a permanent water, pure, translucent, clear, and of a heavenly color:

But where that same thing is not present in the water, by which it is improved, then that which you seek cannot come from it. We therefore hold the Sun in high regard, because our water is improved by it: For without the Sun and its shadow, no <term>tinging</term> <note>from the Latin 'tingere', meaning to dye, stain, or transform the color and nature of a substance</note> Medicine is made.

But whoever believes they can make a <term>Tincture</term> <note>a potent alchemical liquid intended to transform base metals into gold or heal the body</note> without these elements, proceeds to the practice like a donkey reaching for a crown. And this is truly because one body does not work upon another, nor one spirit upon another spirit; the reason being, one form takes no shape from another form, nor one matter from another. Because like does not work upon its like, nor suffer from it, since neither of the two is higher or lower than the other. Therefore, neither among them can work into the other, since an equal has no mastery over that which is equal to it.

But yet, a body does take an image from the spirit, just as matter does from form, because they are suited and convenient to work within and bind to one another. For the body colors, and the spirit penetrates: although a body does not color unless it is first colored itself. For the coarseness of the earth cannot enter in because of its thickness: But the subtle is of an airy nature, which enters and colors, and that is the <term>Sulphur</term> <note>in alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of a substance</note> of the body, drawn out by the spirit.
<margin>x</margin>
Therefore, gold colors nothing but itself, until its hidden spirit is drawn out from its belly, and becomes entirely and completely spiritual.

Our **living water** <note>original: "lebendiges Wasser," likely referring to the alchemical solvent or "mercury of the philosophers"</note> is a fire that burns gold; it kills and breaks it down more than elemental fire does. The more it is mixed with the gold and rubbed together over a gentle fire, the more the gold is broken down and made subtle and thin by this fiery living water. However, after it has been rubbed and brought into a single unity, it possesses all **tinctures** <note>the transformative essence or coloring power of the medicine</note> within itself and can withstand the fire.

When the **body** <note>the solid material, usually gold</note> is thus colored by the **spirit**, it can then give back color in return; it contains and provides every tincture. Therefore, those who make the tincturing **medicine** <note>the Elixir or Stone</note> from the **Sun** <note>gold</note> and its shadow—that is, from the **Moon** <note>silver</note>—are the ones who prepare our **Stone**.

Yet, if the Stone did not possess something of the one essence as well as the other, there would be no interaction or "suffering" <note>the passive reception of influence</note> between them, and one would not tincture the other. For a stone and a piece of wood can in no way act upon one another because they are made of different **matter**; so it is with all things that differ in their material substance.

Therefore, it is evident that it is necessary for the **active** and the **passive** <note>original: "Würkende und Leidende," the agent and the patient</note> to be of only one kind in their genus, but different from one another in their form, just as a woman is different from a man. For although they agree in one genus and nature, they nonetheless have different effects, just as **matter** and **form** agree, as do the active and the passive. For the matter undergoes the action, but the form performs the action
and

and makes the **Matter** <note>original: "Materiam"</note> equal to it. Therefore, **Matter** naturally desires **Form** <note>original: "Formam"</note>, just as the ugly desires the beautiful. Thus the **Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> gladly takes on the **Body**, so that it may reach its **Perfection**.

Therefore, learn to truly know the **mineral roots** if you wish to make your work from them; for we cannot express our **Stone** <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the goal of the alchemical Great Work.</note> by name in any other way, nor call it by other names, but rather we name it to some extent through the description of its roots. And this is done because the **Stone** is called by the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philoſophis"; referring to the ancient and medieval alchemists.</note> "every thing," since it has within itself and from itself everything that is necessary for its **Perfection**.

It is called by every name because of the excellence of its nature, and because of its hidden and various effects. It is found everywhere because it participates in all the **Elements**. It is the most worthless of all things because of its **Putrefaction** <note>The stage of decay or "blackness" in the alchemical process.</note>; yet it is the most precious of all because of its **Power** and **Virtue**. It is black, white, and yellow, according to the transformation of its colors.

Therefore, do not be troubled by the multitude of its names; for no matter what it may be called, it is nonetheless always only one single and identical thing.

In truth, the **Philosophers** do not concern themselves with names, but rather look to the properties of the names, because through one they give the other to be understood; for a thing is not the speech, but the speech is [subservient] to the thing

subjected; that is, the speech must be directed toward the property of the thing.

Accordingly, our **Medicine** <note>The "Medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the alchemical substance in its transformative state.</note> is only one thing in its essence, and uniform in its effect: But yet it is necessary that this very same **Medicine** first be white before it becomes red, because it cannot become red unless it has first been white; for one cannot go from the first to the third except through the second alone. So also there is no path from the Black to the Yellow without passing through the White, for the Yellow is composed of much White and little Black.

Therefore, if you do not first bring this **Medicine** to Whiteness, you will not be able to produce the true Redness afterward.

Thus, the white and red **Matter** are not different in any essence, but only in this: that the red **Medicine** requires a greater **Subtilization** <note>Refining or purifying the substance to a more spiritual or volatile state.</note>, and a longer cooking and heat of the fire in its **Governance** <note>The controlled management of the alchemical furnace and heat levels.</note>; and this is indeed because the end of the work toward the White is a beginning of the work toward the Red. And what is finished and fulfilled in one must be started again in the other. For the entire Masterpiece is begun upon a Whiteness, and also ended. However, the red work must have a proper **Ferment** <note>An agent, often gold for the red work, added to "leaven" or transform the matter.</note>, just as the White requires a white one.

# CHAPTER VI.

Furthermore, in our work there is only one vessel in which the entire masterpiece is completed; it is a "blind flask" <note>A 'blind' flask or *cucurbit* is a vessel without an opening at the top, used for closed circulation.</note> that is not divided in the middle, or a single glass container, thick and strong, sealed everywhere, one cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 to 22 inches.</note> long, round at the top and bottom, without small pores, smooth and wide. Its bottom should be slightly curved or round, but the sides must be even, with the width of a head, so that the substance which is to be sublimated <term>Sublimation: the process where a solid turns into vapor and then back to solid, used here to purify the matter</term> can rise more freely through it. In this way, the fire can be better kindled below and above, wherever it might be necessary.

Any other material is not as good as glass, unless it were of the same substance; for glass alone, and what is equal to glass (because it is a bright, transparent body and without small pores), can hold the volatile spirits <note>original: "flüchtigen Spiritus"; refers to the gaseous or vaporous essences of the chemicals</note> so that they are not driven away by the fire, and it can clearly show the colors that appear during the work, so that the worker does not err in the regulation of the fire.

Other vessels, however, cannot do this, for they have dark bodies full of small pores through which the spirits gradually pass one after another and vanish in smoke. And because it is the property of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurii"; the liquid metal or the philosophical principle of fluidity and volatility</note> to rise through smoke, the air-hole of the vessel must be firmly and constantly sealed, so that

<summary>This chapter describes the specific physical requirements for the alchemical vessel, emphasizing that it must be made of thick, non-porous glass to contain volatile spirits and allow the observer to monitor color changes.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical vessel, glass, sublimation, volatile spirits, Mercury, laboratory equipment, transparency</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the sealing of the vessel and the singular nature of the "work" despite its many names.</meta>

) 0 (

...nothing can go out or fall in through it. For if the air or other foreign moisture were to enter, it would spoil the work; however, if its flower—that is, the white smoke—were to go out and flee away, the entire work would come to nothing and possess no power.

The <term>governance</term><note>German: "Regirung"; refers to the controlled management of heat and conditions during the alchemical process</note> of our Stone, however, is but a single work: namely, that one boils it constantly and perpetually in its vessel until you arrive at the desired end.

Nevertheless, the <term>Philosophers</term><note>The "Philosophers" refers to the historical alchemists and sages who preceded the author</note> have established many artistic terms to honor their art and to conceal it—primarily regarding the purification, so that nothing foul or impure might be added. Such steps are these: mixing, boiling, sublimating, roasting, grinding, hardening or thickening, watering, rotting, whitening, and reddening. These are indeed many names, but the governance is only of one kind—namely, that one only boils. For this reason, the entire work is nothing other than <term>women's work</term><note>A common alchemical metaphor suggesting that once the heat is set, the process requires only patient tending, like laundry or cooking</note> and <term>child's play</term>.

Therefore, grind it, boil it, and repeat this; do not let it vex you to repeat the same many times. For if the Philosophers knew that a single decoction and a single grinding were enough, they would not have repeated their sayings so often; they did this for the reason that one should always grind and boil the compound without ceasing.

But yet, its entire governance consists in

Moderation of the fire, for the distinction of its governance consists in the distinction of the degrees of fire; and to maintain order in the quantity of fire indicates an experienced artist. <note>The "artist" or "adept" refers to the alchemist performing the work.</note> For in the <term>solution</term><note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note> the fire should always be gentle; in the <term>sublimation</term><note>The process where a substance transition from a solid to a gas and back to solid, used for purification.</note> moderate; in the hardening not too strong; in the whitening persistent; but in the reddening <note>The "reddening" or Rubedo is the final stage of the alchemical process, signifying the completion of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> it should be strong. If you should now unknowingly err in this, you will frequently mourn your futile labor.

It is therefore very necessary that you persevere diligently in the work and do not leave it half-finished, for you will gain neither knowledge nor utility from an abandoned work, but rather harm and despair.

It is also especially necessary that you be trained in the science of <term>natural philosophy</term><note>The study of nature and the physical universe that was the precursor to modern science.</note> and endowed with prudence and skill, so that you may quickly remedy any error that occurs through your knowledge; for you would not easily be able to correct such an error through natural diligence alone, since art is aided by understanding, and understanding has help from art.

Likewise, it is also very useful and good that the <term>principles</term><note>original: "Principia"</note> of this art be known to you, and the most prominent roots which belong to the entire work; for he who does not know the beginning will also not find the end.

You should also not strive for how you might find a <term>sophistical</term><note>In alchemy, "sophistic" refers to false or deceptive methods that produce only the appearance of gold rather than the true essence.</note> goal of the work, but your thoughts should be directed solely toward...

So take this rightly to heart, what I have said concerning the arrangement of our Stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting longevity</note>, for therein rests the entire Work. The preparation of our Stone is but of one kind, and it is this: that it be placed into its vessel and be boiled incessantly in the fire until it is entirely dissolved and rises into the heights.

Afterward, the fire must be maintained in the proper measure until the Stone has drawn its moisture back into itself and becomes dry and very white.

Thirdly, the fire must be strengthened until it becomes yellow, and finally very red <note>These color changes—black, white, yellow, and red—represent the traditional stages of the alchemical Great Work: nigredo, albedo, citrinitas, and rubedo</note>. But if you are negligent in the labor, none of these colors will appear to your sight.

Regarding the vessel, you may also take note of this: that the seed must be cast in only once so that a good fruit may grow, and not many times.

And in truth, when one wishes to procreate a human being, or any other creature of the earth, the seed is surely not enclosed in the womb more than once: but if something else were added to it, one would surely spoil the other, either by hindering the ripening, or because air entered therein, or because of an excess of matter. For this reason, women who admit many men to themselves very rarely conceive, or if they do conceive, they bring forth a deformity. Likewise, those who add the raw to the cooked, and the undigested to the digested, do not nourish the body but kill it. For the fruit in the womb is nourished solely by the mother's blood, and lives until it is brought to the light.

We actually dissolve the gold so that it may be brought into its first <term>Matter</term> <note>original: "Materiam"; the raw, undifferentiated substance from which all things arise</note>, that is, that it truly becomes <term>Sulphur</term> <note>The active, combustible principle in alchemy</note> and <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurius"; the fluid, metallic principle</note>; for then we can best make gold and silver from it, when it is converted into the first <term>Matter</term>. And therefore one should wash and cook it, so that it becomes a living <term>Sulphur</term> and <term>Mercury</term>, because these two pieces, according to the opinion of the Sages, are the first <term>Matter</term> of all metals. And certainly our <term>Solution</term> <note>original: "Solutio"; the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note> is nothing other than the body becoming moist again, and the property of <term>Mercury</term> manifesting within it, while the salty taste of its sulfur diminishes. For there is no birth of things, except solely through constant motion, through the exclusion of air, and through tempered warmth.

And an example of this thing can be the womb of a woman; for when she has conceived, the mother <note>The "mother" here refers to the uterus or matrix.</note> is immediately closed, and through the warmth and moisture of the blood, a fruit is begotten. This same fruit, however, never receives external air, except only when it is born.

In the same way, our stone <term>Philosopher's Stone: the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting long life</term> shall always remain closed in its vessel, until it has drawn its moisture into itself, and being perfectly nourished by the warmth of the fire, has become white; then it is born, and the blowing of the air does it no harm.

It is therefore highly necessary to persevere steadily with the work, to maintain a moderate fire, to keep out the air, and especially to do so until it has become white.

Therefore, burn our ore with a gentle fire, just as eggs are brooded upon, until the body is torn apart and the tincture <term>Tincture: the active medicinal or transmuting essence extracted from a substance</term> is drawn out. It is not, however, drawn out all at once, but every day a little goes out, and then a little more, until in the fullness of time it reaches its completion. And that which is dissolved always rises to the height, although that which remains below is greater.

Therefore, always take care that the fire is not too great, so that you do not come to the dissolution before the proper time, and before it is necessary; for such would lead you very far from the right wa—

But that which makes it black is this: it opens the door to the fixed <note>original: "firen" — refers to stable, non-volatile substances</note> and transforms the fixed with the volatile. While it tortures and torments it, this does not happen with harm or destruction, but rather with union and utility. For if its torment were harmful and improper, it would not be accepted by it, nor would it extract its colors, which we call the water of sulfur <note>original: "Schweffels" — in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible principle of soul and spirit</note>.

But nevertheless, that which is first black, we call the key to the entire work, because it cannot be accomplished without blackness. For this same blackness is the tincture <note>term: Tincture — a potent medicinal or alchemical substance capable of transforming or "coloring" other matter</note> that we strive for, with which we tinge every body <note>original: "Corpus"</note>; this blackness was previously hidden in its ore, just as the soul is hidden in the human body.

Therefore, if our ore is not broken, moistened, and ground, and diligently governed by itself until it is drawn out from its thickness and converted into a thin and incomprehensible spirit <note>original: "Spiritum"</note>, then all labor is in vain.

For if your bodies are not transformed so that they are no longer bodies, and the incorporeal are made into bodies, then you have not yet found the right path of the work. This is because we cannot extract the most subtle soul, which contains the entire tincture within itself, from its body unless it is first broken and converted into a subtle and incomprehensible spirit.

Also, a body <note>In alchemy, "body" usually refers to a solid metal or mineral base</note> whose parts are tightly bound together will not be dissolved, unless it happens with fire and water. But our water is a fire, which burns the bodies more than common fire. Therefore, whoever governs this water by itself can extract from it a nature which surpasses all natures. For that reason, be a diligent worker in every state; persevere with patience in the boiling, until the entire tincture <note>The essential soul or color-giving property of the substance</note> comes out over the water, in color like a molten pitch. And when you see the blackness standing over the water, then you shall know that the body has perished and melted away.

### CHAPTER VIII.

Afterward, one must constantly persevere with a gentle fire until it has acquired a dark mist. For the opinion of the philosophers <note>original: "Philosophorum"; referring to the "Sons of Art" or historical alchemists</note> is that the body, having been dissolved into a black powder, now enters again into its water and becomes entirely one thing. But if you were to ask and say: "What kind of powder will come from water?" then know that this same powder is nothing other than a water of sulfur, which is dissolved through the heat of the fire. Therefore, by right, this water accepts the other as its own nature. Consequently, where everything is not turned into water, you will by no means come to perfection <note>The completion of the Great Work</note>, for one must absolutely not use anything else in the mixing,

It emphasizes that the transition to a black powder is a necessary step toward achieving the final "perfection" of the work.</summary>

<meta>Continuity from page 142: The text continues discussing the alchemical process of dissolution and the nature of the "permanent water." Catchword "ches" from the previous page likely completed the word "Forschung" (investigation/research) or "Mischung" (mixture).</meta>

# ❦ ) o ( ❦ 143

investigation, in the <term>trituration</term><note>The process of grinding or rubbing a solid substance to a fine powder to facilitate chemical change.</note>, and in the entire governance, as this permanent water alone. And note that its power is a spiritual essence <note>original: "Geblüte" - literally "blood-essence" or "flowering," referring to the vital life-force of the substance</note>, without which nothing can be made.

It is, however, transformed into a body, and the body is made into a spirit by that very same thing: For when they are thus mixed with one another and brought into one, then one transforms the other: Since the body makes the spirit <term>corporeal</term><note>Physical or bodily in nature.</note>, but the spirit transforms the body into a spirit as red as blood. For everything that has a spirit also has blood: Therefore, that same blackness which floats upon their water must be driven further and further with a gentle fire, until it sinks to the bottom of its water and becomes a water within the water—that is, until it all becomes water together.

Now, when one water is mixed with the other, then one water accepts the other, so that they cannot be separated from one another.

But the unwise, when they hear of "water," think that it is a water from the clouds: But if they had understanding, they should rightly know that it is a permanent or <term>fixed water</term><note>A substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to heat.</note>, which nevertheless cannot remain stable without its body, with which it is dissolved and has become one thing.

The Philosophers, however, have called this water a "Water of the Sun," a "fiery poison," and "the good thing that has many names." Therefore, when we [seek] this water, which...

<summary>This page describes the alchemical union of body and spirit, explaining how they transform each other into a "permanent water" through heat and trituration. It warns the reader not to mistake this philosophical "water" for common rainwater, identifying it instead as a stable, solar essence.</summary>

### CHAPTER IX.

Whoever now knows how to marry, to make pregnant, to give birth, to kill, and to make alive, to bring in a light, and further to cleanse it of its blackness <note>original: "Schwärze," referring to the Nigredo stage of the alchemical process</note> and darkness, he shall be in high honor. For when we lay our crowned son <note>A symbolic term for purified Gold or a perfected metallic spirit</note> beside our red daughter <note>Likely referring to refined Mercury or a sulfurous base</note>, and unite them with a gentle fire, then she will conceive and bear a son. For the wet clouds that were above her will return again into her body, from whence they came. It is therefore necessary,

It is necessary that you decompose the <term>coloring agent</term> <note>original: "tingirende"</note> and that which is to be colored. For that which is mixed with it overcomes it and brings it to its own color: And just as it visibly overcomes it in the upper part, so it also overcomes the internal parts. Therefore, when mixed with the body and retained by it so that it may be colored with it, it transforms the same into a <term>Spirit</term> <note>Latin: "Spiritum"; the volatile or gaseous essence of a substance</note>, and colors it with a spiritual and unchangeable <term>Tincture</term> <note>The potent medicinal or transformative essence</note> which cannot be destroyed.

Now, if you allow them to govern one another continuously for 40 days, they will both become a constant water, covered with a blackness. This blackness, however, if governed in the proper manner, does not remain longer than 41 days: Therefore, govern them in their bath, make a fire beneath them until it becomes a clear water, and like a true <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Quecksilber"; here referring to the philosophical principle of fluidity and volatility</note> which rises into the air. Therefore, when you see that the natures become water and rise into the height, then they have altogether become a vapor. For after the soul has departed from the body and has been made into a spirit through <term>Sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it vapors and then solidifies again, used here to describe spiritual purification</note>, they have both become volatile: For the water has unlocked the door for the <term>fixed</term> <note>The solid, stable part of the matter</note> and has transformed it into a <term>Spirit</term> that is equal to it: Hence they have become joyful spirits, which have risen together into the air and have there received the life that was breathed into them by their moisture, just as man has life from the air, whereby he in-

Thus, one vapor rightly holds the other, because both are placed together simultaneously in the <term>cooking<note>original: "Kochung" – the process of applying steady heat to a substance to mature or "ripen" it.</note></term>. Therefore the volatile nature, although its essence is nothing other than volatile, has nevertheless ceased to be volatile, because in the <term>sublimation<note>original: "Sublimation" – the process where a solid turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to describe the purification of the spirit.</note></term> one has been joined to the other.

For this reason, the entire work should be frequently sublimated into a vapor by a moderate fire, so that it may be breathed upon by the air and be able to live. For the life of all natural things consists of the breathing in of air. Therefore, the entire work consists in the vapor and sublimation of its water.

But you must take the greatest care in every sublimation that your glass or your vessel is not broken. For if you were to kindle the fire so much that the water had to rise to the uppermost part of the vessel, it would rejoice in the cooling and cling there, and thus you would not be able to perform the sublimation of the elements; the reason being, that each among them must be brought down and up again frequently by its own <term>circulated driving-around<note>original: "circulirtes Herumtreiben" – referring to the alchemical process of circulation where vapors rise, condense, and flow back down in a continuous cycle.</note></term>. But what rises up by force does not come down again without force.

Therefore your fire should be gentle, so that the entire work may rise and descend freely of its own accord, without clinging to the vessel. For this reason, where we do not [treat] the body with water

It warns against excessive heat that forces substances to stick to the top of the vessel, disrupting the essential cycle of rising and falling.</summary>

and make the fire thin, until it rises as a **Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritus"; in alchemy, this refers to a volatile substance or the "breath" of a metal</note>; otherwise we achieve nothing.
But when it rises into the air, it is born, and transformed into air, and becomes a life, so that one cannot be separated from the other, just as one cannot separate water that has been mixed with other water. Therefore, that which is born in the air is born whitish, since it is born entirely and completely spiritual.

Now note that we perform **Sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the material</note> for three reasons: The first is that the **Body** <note>original: "Cörper"; the solid, physical matter of the alchemical work</note> may become a spirit of subtle nature: The second is that the spirit may incorporate itself with the body and become one with it: The third is that everything may become pure and white, and the saltiness of the **Sulfur** <note>original: "Schweffels"; one of the three primary alchemical principles, representing the soul and combustibility</note> may be diminished: For through sublimation, whatever is combustible in the work burns away.

And in truth, it is highly necessary for us that the **Elements** <note>original: "Elementa"</note> become simple, so that they can be composed together; yet they cannot become simple unless they are separated into their parts: Therefore, one must sublimate both vapors many times, until the water descends in the sifting.

But we sift the things in the round-spherical sieve, seven times, so that everything becomes a bright, clear water, since the body never allows the **Soul** <note>original: "Seel"</note> to be separated from it, which is like it in simple transformation, and therefore we repeat their sublimation, so that they may be made into a subtle nature.

One must therefore carry out the separation <term>Scheidung; the alchemical process of isolating components</term> according to one's ability, so that nothing of the oiliness of the souls remains in the body, except only what is not noticed during its elevation; otherwise, it will be hindered in its operation.

For it is sifted through the sieve of the round sphere, one after the other, and the body settles to the bottom, and the water rises up white with the simple, kindred soul.

So always pour one part together with the other, so that when they are rectified <note>purified or refined through repeated distillation</note> they can withstand the elements, and especially the fire: For that which does not withstand the resisting force will be separated and diminish. Therefore, the body must be dry and parched, the soul moist, and the spirit flowing.

However, some believe that the separation happens in many different ways; you should not concern yourself with that, for in this you will succeed: that you bring the work to a simple equality, and not in such a way that each thing is separated individually into the elements.

And certainly, it cannot be difficult to bring everything to a simple whiteness, because they are of its nature: Its father is the Sun, its mother the Moon, the wind has carried it in its belly, the earth has become pregnant by it <note>original: "Sein Vater ist die Sonne, seine Mutter der Mond..." This is a direct reference to the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, a foundational alchemical text</note>, because you have obtained the water from the air, the air from the fire, and the fire from the earth, through distilling. For through frequent repetition of sublimation <term>Sublimation; the process where a solid turns into vapor and then back to solid, used here to purify the "soul"</term>, the soul, which rises with the water, is purified, and its coarseness, which descends into the earth, is polished.

Thus you shall now make the Earth from the Fire, and the Water from the Air, which you shall then bring back over the Earth. For its power is complete when it has been turned back into the Earth. Therefore, turn the Earth into Water, the Water into Fire, and the Fire into an Air; hide the Fire in the innermost Earth, and the Earth in the belly of the Air. You must mix the Warm with the Moist, and the Dry with the Cold, because in such a manner you can create a mixture; for one cannot go from one end to the other without passing through the middle <note>This refers to the Aristotelian theory of elements, where transformations must happen through shared qualities (e.g., Fire and Air share Heat).</note>.

Therefore, the Eagle that flies through the air and the Toad that creeps across the earth are the masterpiece <note>The Eagle represents volatile substances or vapors, while the Toad represents the fixed, solid matter at the bottom of the vessel.</note>. For this reason, separate the Earth from the Fire, the subtle from the dense, gently and with great understanding.

It ascends from the Earth into the Heaven, and descends again into the Earth, and receives the upper power of the Spirit and the lower power of the Body, because it overcomes all subtle things through <term>Coagulation</term> <note>The process of turning a liquid or gas into a solid state.</note>, and it penetrates all solid things through transformation. For in such a form it rules over the upper and the lower, because its effect works as well upon the spirits as it does upon the bodies.

Thus it is now manifest that our Ore has a body, spirit, and soul, just like a human being. The spirit is its water, the soul is the...

The soul, however, is a bond of the spirit, just as the body is a bond of the soul: the body is fixed and dry, and retains the spirit and the soul.

Thus the spirit penetrates, the body makes fixed, and the soul binds together, tints, and makes white. But whoever cannot make two <term>Sulfurs</term><note>original: "Schweffel"; in alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle</note> out of one Sulfur in this work knows nothing: and there are two Sulfurs which are sublimated into the Tincture from the mixed and compounded Stone; these indeed tint and would fly away, but they are held by the Sulfur so that they cannot flee, because it binds the volatile elements together. Yet in these three there is a smoke, a blackness, and death, which, if they are not removed, the others will not be permanent.

Therefore, one must drive away the smoke from the water, the blackness from the soul, and death from the body through frequent <term>dissolution</term><note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state to purify it</note>. Therefore, mix the eggs of the black hens with ore, and you shall have as much gold and silver as you desire. But the vulture who flies over the mountain without wings cries out: I am the whiteness of the black, the redness of the white, and the yellowness of the red, a true son who does not lie: therefore place me with the mother and at her breast, for I make her substance permanent.

Therefore, you must bring nothing foreign in to us, and you must not cease from the Work <note>The "Great Work" or Magnum Opus of alchemy</note>, for every nature is united with its companion and is made perfect through it. The Mother has begotten me, and she shall be born through me. For she ruled over me at first, but henceforth I shall rule over her, because I have become a pursuer of my Mother after I received from her the ability to fly <note>Likely referring to sublimation or the volatile state</note>. Nevertheless, she, as a pious mother, cherishes, tends, and nourishes me, her son, whom she has begotten, in the best way she can, until I can reach the state of perfection.

Therefore, place me in a moist fire <note>A gentle, indirect heat like a water bath or "balneum Mariae"</note>, which increases the warmth of my moisture and prevents the burning of dryness, until it brings the work to its end. After that, draw out the redness and my shadow for itself, until I consist of the third part. For the decoction <note>The process of boiling or heating</note> makes me lesser, but that which is ground increases it. And what is diminished after 15 days is increased after 30 days; that is the beginning and the end.

Consequently, preserve the mercury in the innermost part of its chamber, in which it has become hard; for from the oft-repeated sifting, a white water rises with the soul to the helm <note>The top part of a still or alembic</note>, and the body gradually descends to the bottom. And this same thing is mercury, which is called the "remnant earth," which accepts the water and drinks it into itself, because it is a union of the tinctures.
