## CHAPTER XI.

Therefore, bind the hands of the nursing woman behind her back, so that she cannot escape from Gabricus <note>original: "Gaabrico"; in alchemy, Gabricus represents the male principle or sulfur, often paired with Beya, the female principle</note>, and place the son she has conceived at her breast so that she may nurse him. For when the woman has died, the toad will become thick with milk. Then, cut the toad in half and throw it to the hen to eat; once she has consumed the toad, she will be white both inside and out.

You must bring the water back down to the earth and govern it with a moderate fire until it has taken root in its own nature. Know for certain that it must first be nourished with a little milk, just as one sees in the raising of a child; for it is nourished with a small amount of milk and warmed by a small fire as long as it is small. The more it grows, the more food and heat it requires, until it has swallowed its own moisture. For the first moisture is cold; therefore, one must guard against an excessively large fire, which is contrary to the cold.

However, if you place the body <note>original: "Cörper"; the solid material in the alchemical vessel</note> upon the fire without vinegar, it will be scorched, and that which is sought after will not come of it. Since the fire will find no moisture to dry out, the body burns. But the vinegar that is added to it prevents the burning of the fire and dries itself along with the body so that it suffers no harm. ## CHAPTER XII.

The water, however, must be divided into two parts, of which the first is <term>coagulated<note>original: "coaguliret"; the process of a liquid becoming solid</note></term> into the body, and the other becomes <term>putrefied<note>original: "faul"; refers to the breaking down or rotting of the substance</note></term> and dissolves. flee. In this hunt you must be slow, and take care
that they are not transformed into smoke by too
great a fire: for if they escape, they can be caught
again with no falcon: but if they are enclosed and do
not flee, then they return to the body from which
they departed; the reason being, the body draws its
moisture to itself, just as the magnet draws iron.

Therefore, transform the earths, boil off the
water, and repeat the work until one of the
ternaries is turned downward. For when you have
killed one of the three, they are all dead; since,
where one is volatile but the other is stable in the
fire, when they are both placed together, they
endure the fire, because one retains the other
due to the close kinship of its nature; and this
indeed is our <term>coagulation</term><note>The process of turning a liquid or volatile substance into a solid state.</note>, for everything volatile is
prevented from flight by its <term>fixed</term><note>A substance that is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note> part.

But yet this does not happen with the hands; rather, Nature works therein in a circular manner <note>original: "Circulsweise," referring to the alchemical cycle of distillation and condensation</note>, because Nature knows herself. She adorns herself with red color, she makes herself yellow and black; she weds herself and conceives from herself until she brings the work to its conclusion. From this it is to be seen that this preparation is not a manual labor, but a transformation of the natures, and a wonderful union of the warm with the cold, as well as the moist with the dry. Hence these rhymes:

From a hidden spring a clear water flows,
Which pours itself out into twelve arms like veins;
When this is observed by the warm fire,
And becomes hard, then it makes the dead living.
It washes away, makes pure, renews and governs,
Thereto it unites, and thereby gives new birth:
The earth grows green from it, bringing forth beautiful flowers,
Which with their scent give joy entirely.

# ) o (

In this work, it is necessary that neither an all-too-great dryness nor an excessive amount of moisture should ruin it; rather, one must boil away through roasting exactly as much as was added during the dissolution, and through moistening, one must *dissolve* <note>original: "dissolvire"</note> as much as was taken away during the roasting.

At all times, however, one must prevent a harsh fire from causing a burning, and one must not cease in the work until it takes on the form of a dry stone entirely.

Now, nature has no movement except through the effect of heat: Therefore, if you measure the heat well, then water and fire can be enough for your work, for they wash away, purify, and nourish the body, and take away its darkness. And in truth, the entire work and its governance <note>term: "Regierung" (governance) refers to the controlled management of the alchemical furnace and the chemical process over time</note> is nothing other than a constant water, which has within itself everything that we require. For it makes soft and it makes hard, it breaks apart and it makes white, and one does not need to use anything else in the entire governance except for this permanent water. Therefore, protect and preserve this water with its effect, since it makes white for the White, and red for the Red <note>This refers to the two main stages of the Great Work: the Albedo (whiteness) and the Rubedo (redness)</note>.

Yet its effect consists in the governance of the fire. For through this, the water is divided; through this, it is prevented from fleeing <note>original: "von der Flucht abgehalten," referring to preventing the volatile spirits from evaporating</note>; through this, the soul is joined to the body; and through this, our *coagulation* <note>original: "Coagulatio"; the process of turning a liquid into a solid</note> occurs.

It explains that fire governs the process by fixing volatile elements and uniting the soul with the body of the matter.</summary>

Therefore, it is not in vain that the seed is brought into the mother, since one follows the other, just as the bride follows the bridegroom. ### CHAPTER XV.

Now observe how the *composite* <note>original: "Compositum"; the mixture of substances in the alchemical vessel</note> drinks in the water, and how it changes in color at every degree. For when the thing is placed upon the fire, it changes its colors manifoldly, from one to another, through the operation of nature.

Yet the spirit and the soul are not united with the body, except only in the white color. For as long as the dark blackness lets itself be seen, the woman rules <note>In alchemical allegory, the "woman" or "feminine" principle often represents the cold, moist, or lunar aspect of the matter</note>, and she is indeed the first power of our stone; for if it does not become black, it can also not become white nor red: For the redness is composed of black and white together. Moreover, when a tempered heat works into the moist, it creates the blackness, dries out the moisture, and takes away the corruption.

On the contrary, if the heat becomes too strong, it causes the redness, drives out the moisture, and brings about corruption <note>The author warns that excessive heat destroys the delicate balance required for the "Great Work"</note>.

From this it is evident, that where the *composite* is driven more than is proper, it is spoiled immediately, and that which is sought and desired cannot come of it. Therefore, one must take great care that the water through

### CHAPTER XVI.

Honor, therefore, the King and his Wife <note>In alchemy, the King and Queen or Wife usually represent the masculine and feminine principles, such as Sulfur and Mercury</note>, and do not burn them, lest they flee from the great heat; for you have need of patience and long-suffering, which improve the King and his consort in their governance. Cook them until they become black, then white, then red, and then it will become a <term>Medicine</term> <note>Arzney: here referring to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone</note> that <term>tinctures</term> <note>tingiret: to transform or dye the base metal</note> into gold.

For our ore, the more it is cooked at the beginning, the more it is <term>dissolved</term> <note>solviret: from the Latin solvere, to break down into liquid</note> and becomes a spiritual water. After that, the more it is cooked, the thicker it becomes, and it becomes a very white powder. Thirdly, the more it is cooked, the more it is colored, and it becomes a <term>Tincture</term> <note>Tincktur: the final transformative substance</note> of a deep redness; and this entire work is nothing else but the extraction of water from the earth, and the pouring of the same back onto the earth. However, there must be diligent care and continuous hope, for everything will explain itself, and thus your desire will finally be achieved.

So now, let your water—which has been completely extracted and is found within the earth—remain for several days (that is, for about 60 days) in its vessel over a gentle fire to reach <term>putrefaction</term> <note>Fäule: the stage of decay or "blackness" necessary for rebirth in the alchemical process</note>, until the precious white color appears at the top.

The heat, however, should be gentle until the whiteness appears; for if too great a heat is applied suddenly...

If it were to creep in, it creates a black vapor, and the white <term>compound</term><note>original: "Compositum"; the combined alchemical substances</note> flees from it: For that same vapor is white, therefore when it is <term>fixed</term><note>rendered solid or non-volatile</note> with the ore, it makes the same white inside and out. But where it flees from it, it becomes red, which is of no use.

And in truth, in the <term>putrefaction</term><note>the process of decay or fermentation intended to "kill" the old form of the metal</note> of the ore, the spirits are united with the body, and made dry with the same: Which indeed, if they are not made dry, the colors of the souls cannot appear. For putrefaction is nothing other than a killing of the moist with the dry. For the moist is entirely <term>coagulated</term><note>thickened or solidified</note> with the dry or through the dry.

The common man putrefies through moisture alone: But our putrefaction does not happen without the moist and the dry, because the moisture is kept solely within the dry, so that it cannot escape.

Since heavy things cannot be raised up without the presence of light things, nor can light things be brought down without the company of heavy ones; and both are the beginning and the end of the work.

Govern your work, therefore, with persistence; cook without ceasing, yet you must not hurry, nor stop in the labor, for if the moisture of the mother and the heat did not endure forever, the seed could not remain, nor a fruit become perfect. For if the mother dies, the fruit of the womb also dies because of the penetrating cold. Therefore has

God ordained the blood and the warmth of the mother to nourish the fruit of the womb until the time of birth.
    In the same manner, you must also constantly govern your work with warmth until it reaches the white color.     Therefore, it begins to live at that point, and the external air does not harm it, unless you are willing to proceed further to the red color.
    A bath of very violent heat destroys it, while cold drives it away; but a tempered heat helps it along and preserves it. Therefore, one must govern it gently, as in the brooding of eggs, until it is entirely white. Hence it is commanded that one should make the *Laton* <note>original: "Latonem"; a term for impure metal or "copper" that must be cleansed to reach the silver stage</note> white, and "tear up the books" <note>a common alchemical metaphor suggesting that once the white stage is reached, further complex instructions are unnecessary, or that the truth is now plain to see</note> so that hearts are not torn.

Then the dark dominion of the woman passes away; then the man rises above the woman and takes the kingdom from her. Then the smoke penetrates into the body, and the spirit is bound in the dryness. Then the blackness—that misshapen color—ceases, and it becomes white, bright, and clear.

...and in the white, the yellow color. One can find an example of this in burning wood when one makes charcoal: The same is true for the <term>substance of our stone</term><note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary goal of alchemy believed to transmute base metals into gold and grant long life.</note>, for first, the more it is cooked, the more it is dissolved and becomes black. Afterward, the more it is cooked, the more it is dried out and becomes white. Thirdly, the more it is cooked, the more red it becomes, until it is made perfect. Now, the thing which has a red head, white feet, but black eyes—that is the masterpiece.

## CHAPTER XVII.

There are, nevertheless, four principal colors that reveal themselves in the work, namely: Black, White, Golden-Yellow, and Red. And all the names of the <term>ore</term><note>original: "Erzes"; here referring to the alchemical matter or "prime material" being worked upon.</note> are derived from these four colors that appear in its body, all of which arise from the <term>vinegar</term><note>original: "Essig"; in alchemy, this often refers to a specialized acidic solvent or "mercurial water" rather than common table vinegar.</note> that is poured upon it.

But when the aforementioned colors appear, one must cook each of them for forty days; and when all the water is consumed, make it dry with a fine, gentle heat until the whiteness comes forth. However, it is provable that it is necessary for the work to first become black, because no birth happens except through the destruction of another. And this <term>corruption</term><note>original: "Corruptio"; the breaking down of the initial matter.</note> does not happen except through <term>putrefaction</term><note>original: "Putrefaction"; the stage of rotting or decomposition necessary for rebirth.</note> and through the heat acting upon the moisture. And the heat acting upon the moisture first produces a blackness: from which it can be seen that the beginning of our work is the <term>Raven’s Head</term><note>original: "Rabenhaupt" or "Caput Corvi"; a famous alchemical symbol for the "Nigredo" or black stage of the process.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the sequential color changes in the alchemical process—black, white, yellow, and red—likening the transformation to the burning of wood. Likewise, it is also necessary that it <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: "putrescire"; the process of breaking down or decaying to release inner potential</note>. For nothing was ever born, nor grown, nor became living, except after <term>putrefaction</term>. For if it does not become rotten, one cannot pour it, nor can it be <term>dissolved</term> <note>original: "solviren"</note>; but where it is not dissolved, it comes to nothing. Yet our <term>putrefaction</term> is not filthy or unclean, but is rather a mixing of the earth with the water, and the water with the earth, through and through, until it all becomes one <term>body</term> <note>original: "Corpus"</note>. Therefore, boil the <term>little man</term> <note>the masculine/sulfuric principle</note> and his <term>wife</term> <note>the feminine/mercurial principle</note> together until they both become hard and dry. For if it does not become dry, the various colors will by no means appear; the reason being that it will always be black as long as the moisture prevails.

Therefore, the mouth of the vessel shall be diligently sealed, and boil it by the fire until everything settles downward. Then the <term>dragon</term> <note>a symbol for the volatile or raw matter undergoing change</note> eats his wings and gives off various colors, inasmuch as he is transformed many and various times from one color into another, until he comes to the constant <term>whiteness</term>. For all the colors in the world will come forth when the blackening moisture has been dried out. But you must not pay great heed to such colors, because they are not true colors; for it often becomes gold-yellow and red, and becomes dry and soft again many times before the true <term>whiteness</term>. The <term>spirit</term> <note>the volatile essence</note>, however, does not become fixed with the <term>body</term> except only in the white color.

Therefore, there is no doubt that one must all—

Wait for the time until it becomes white, because the white color is the fulfillment of the entire work: And after that, it does not transform into any other true color except for red.

Thus, the colors will surely teach you how you should behave with the fire, since they will indicate to you at what time and for how long you should maintain the fire of the first, second, and third degrees. Hence these rhymes:

> The red vein has come forth from the clear well,
> Since the sun's heat has taken its moisture away:
> You must cook it gently until it becomes dry,
> And thereafter white; then your earth is perfected.

Therefore, if you moderate the fire well and conduct your work correctly, you will first arrive at the white color. But when a dry heat is maintained at the top and bottom for several days, it will be completely fixed at the bottom, and no more spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"; the volatile, gaseous essence of the matter</note> will rise up, which is a sign of a perfect fixation. Concerning this are these rhymes:

> He who does not wish to strictly observe the rule of the fire,
> Cannot strive for high things in this work.
> When you see a smoke gradually rising up,
> Ensure that it takes its course downward again.

At that time, the volatile shall not fly away from that which is not volatile, even if the fire is very hot. And that is the very best thing that laborers in <term>Alchemy</term><note>original: "Alchimia"</note> can take to transform substances into silver: one nature then retains the other, and in a true marriage, they are transformed one into the other.

It is, nevertheless, nothing other than one single correct nature, which in every degree of its operation is converted into another nature; for one nature rejoices in the other, one nature overcomes the other, and one nature holds the other and teaches it to fight against the fire. And yet these are not distinct or multiple natures, but it is only one nature alone, which has the property and all things within itself that are necessary to it; therefore, the work is begun and ended in one single way.

But there is a twofold manner of composition, for one is moist, while the other is dry; however, when they are cooked, they become one single thing, so that neither of them may leave the other. And this is quite right, for the moist easily takes on a form <note>original: "Bild" - can mean image, shape, or form</note> but also loses it easily. The dry, however, accepts an impression <note>original: "Abtruck"</note> with difficulty, but also lets go of it with great difficulty.

Therefore, when the moist and the dry have been tempered <term>tempered: balanced or blended in the correct proportion</term> with one another, the dry easily receives a form from the moist, and the moist gains from the dry the ability to hold that form firmly and withstand all fire. Thus, for the sake of the moisture, the dry is prevented from separating itself, and for the sake of the dry, the moist is prevented from being able to flow any longer.

An example of this is the work of a potter, who binds the earth with water and cooks them by the fire, so that one does not let go of the other.

In this same way, create your masterpiece <note>original: "Meisterstück" - referring to the Great Work of alchemy</note>, and govern the earth only with moisture and heat until they unite and equalize, and are no longer contrary to one another, nor separate. Then apply to them two powerful effects <note>original: "Würkungen"</note>, the water and the fire; then the masterpiece will be completed.

For if you mix in the water alone, it will become white; but if you add the fire to it, it will become red.

For the white is fulfilled in three rotations <note>likely referring to cycles of distillation or "circulations" of the matter</note> in which there is no fire; the red

For when the Body is made white, it does not let the <term>Spirit</term><note>original: "Spiritum"; the volatile, gaseous, or "soul" element of a substance.</note> fly away, nor does it allow the blackness to show itself any further. The soul, however, is the bond of the spirit, just as the body is the bond of the soul, and this bond is not like the bond of nature, but rather as a composite.

For the <term>composition</term><note>original: "Compositio"; the structural arrangement or joining of elements.</note> of nature consists in this: that two opposing things come together; but our composition consists in this: that we fix composite things. But whoever does not make the body fluid with the spirit, so that it becomes a water that one can hardly feel, and afterwards <term>coagulates</term><note>original: "coaguliret"; to thicken or solidify a liquid.</note> the same over a gentle fire, errs in many ways.

Therefore, make the earth black through the separation of its souls; thereafter, bring the water back over the earth by itself, and gradually make it white and dry, and you shall have the entire masterpiece. ## CHAPTER XVIII.

Thus you have now, dear friend, learned to make the white work; now you must also know how to prepare the red. But yet, if you do not make it white, you will not be able to make the true red, just as no one can come from the first to the third except through the second. In the same manner, you cannot come from the black to the yellow without passing through the white; for the yellow is composed of much white and little black. Therefore, make the black white, and make the white red, and you shall have the masterpiece.

For just as the year is divided into four seasons, so also is our blessed work. The first season is winter, moist and cold, when it rains much; the second season is cold and dry—

...bring forth flowers. The third is the **Summer**, warm and dry, reddish. The fourth is the **Autumn**, the time to gather the fruits. In such an order do the <term>tincturing natures</term><note>The transformative chemical properties of the alchemical substance as it matures.</note> rule, until they become ripe and bring forth fruit according to your desire. But the winter is now past, the rain is gone, and the night has retreated. The flowers have now come forth in our land at springtime; but we rest upon the **white rose** alone, because it has the power to transform all weak bodies into true silver.

When you see that the **Whiteness** comes forth, you may be certain that the **Redness** is hidden within it; and then one must not take out that same Whiteness, but rather cook it until it becomes entirely red. And the redder it is, the more powerful it is, because it tinctures more abundantly. Thus, that which is cooked more is also redder; from this it follows that what is cooked more also has more power, and consequently, that which is cooked the most can also accomplish the most.

And in truth, the red color is caused solely by a perfect <term>ripening</term><note>Original: "Zeitigung." In alchemy, this refers to the slow maturation of the matter through heat, similar to fruit ripening on a tree.</note>. For no blood is produced in man unless it be diligently cooked in the liver. Likewise, in the morning when we see that our urine is white, and we know that we have slept little, we return again to bed. But when we have slept again, the digestion is fully completed, and the urine becomes yellow.

from the **Whiteness** <note>The *albedo* stage of the alchemical process</note> you shall arrive at the **Redness**, provided that you cannot err if you continue to persist with him using **dry fire**. In the same manner, our **white ore**, if it is not diligently cooked, will not turn red. And certainly, the warmth of the liver alone makes the digested juice red, just as the ignited heat of the fire alone makes the **white sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphur*</note> red. For the first **digestion** in the stomach makes everything white, but the second in the liver makes everything red; for in the liver alone is the heat powerful, just as in the stomach the dryness is powerful.

Therefore, with dry fire and dry **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances or effect oxidation</term>, cook the dry matter until it becomes red like **cinnabar** <note>a bright red mineral, mercury sulfide</note>, to which you must by no means add water or anything else, until it becomes completely red through the cooking. Therefore, you should burn it in the dry fire without fear, until it is clothed with a very red color. You should not desist from the work, even if it might linger a little while before the redness emerges. For when the fire is increased after the whiteness, you will obtain the redness from the principal colors; yet between these two colors, the **yellow** will show itself.

### CHAPTER XIX.

When you have now brought forth every kind from the parts of the work, as we have indicated how to govern the same, then bring the **Spirits** <term>Spiritus: The volatile parts of a substance that can be raised by heat.</term> back to it again, and **sublime** <term>Sublimation: The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then solidifies again, used here to purify the material.</term> it together, for it will sublime itself bright and clear, so that it does not allow itself to be mixed with the elements.

And the same indeed behaves in such a way, that you take the very purest part of the **Stone**, and compose the same through and through in a subtle, ingenious manner, so it will rise up entirely. Where that does not happen, one must add something to it from the lower part, so that the size of the—

The quantity of the volatile <note>The "volatile" refers to substances that evaporate or sublimate easily when heated, often associated with the "Spirit."</note> must surpass the size of the fixed <note>The "fixed" refers to stable matter that resists the fire.</note>, until it is sufficient for its sublimation.
For we have observed in the manner we used in our experience, that when something is mixed with water, and the quantity of the volatile exceeds the quantity of the fixed, the fixed then flees away with the former; but where it does not exceed it, it becomes fixed <term>figiret: stabilized or made solid</term> with it.
For the spirit will carry up into the heights whatever has been added to it. Therefore, it will all the sooner carry with it that which is entirely of its own nature.

However, one must perform this sublimation with dry heat. And when it is sublimated, this must be repeated one after the other, until through such sublimation and its repetition it becomes entirely fixed. Now, when it is fixed, you must soak the fixed with an equal part of the un-fixed, one after the other according to the art known to you, until it rises up again. Therefore, it must be fixed once more entirely, until it lets itself be poured easily, like wax.

And this is now the constant medicine, which tinctures <term>tingiret: dyes or transforms the inner nature of a metal</term>, penetrates, and does not perish, of which one part transforms a thousand and more parts of any body into the best gold and silver, according to whether the elixir has been prepared as white or red.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of balancing volatile and fixed substances through repeated sublimation and "soaking" to create a stable, wax-like "Medicine" or Elixir. This final substance is claimed to have the power to transmute base metals into gold or silver depending on its color.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, fixation, volatile spirit, alchemy, elixir, transmutation, gold, silver, tincture</keywords>

## CHAPTER XX.

The quality of this **multiplication** <note>original: "Vermehrung"; the process of increasing the potency and quantity of the alchemical agent</note> comes from nowhere else than the manifold and repeated **sublimation** <note>the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified solid deposit</note> and **shaping** <note>original: "Figurung"; likely referring to the fixing or stabilizing of the matter into a specific form</note> of this **medicine**: For the more often the order of this work is repeated, the more abundantly it is increased, and its power and **perfection** become so great that it transforms all imperfect bodies—even **mercury** <note>original: "Quecksilber"; often considered the most difficult "imperfect" metal to fix</note>—into true gold and silver in countless amounts. For as often as you **sublimate** and **dissolve** <note>original: "solviren"</note> the perfect medicine, you will always gain in the **projection** <note>the final stage where the medicine is cast upon base metals to transform them</note> by adding one part to a thousand parts: So that if it first falls upon a thousand parts, the second time it can be applied to ten thousand, the third to a hundred thousand, the fourth to a thousand times a thousand, and the fifth to uncountable parts.

Therefore, the more subtle the **elixir** is made, the more it **tinctures** <note>original: "tingiret"; the act of staining or transforming the color and nature of a metal</note>, and indeed, the more you sublimate your work, the more power it will have, as it will work more abundantly and transform a greater quantity.

And that is exactly what **Geber** <note>Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a legendary 8th-century alchemist regarded as a primary authority in the Middle Ages</note> says, who was a master over all masters in our art:

> Go to work with your **stone**, and with my stone, and my stone shall have more power than yours: For if you cast yours upon a thousand, I will cast mine upon 10,000, and all shall be well. But if you cast upon 10,000, I will cast one part upon a thousand times a thousand. Therefore

## CHAPTER XXI.

Now you shall perform your <term>Projection</term> <note>The final stage of the Great Work, where the Alchemical Stone is cast upon base metals to transmute them.</note> in this manner: First, multiply 10 by 10, which will be 100; and 100 by 100, which will be 1,000; and 1,000 by 1,000, which will be ten thousand; and ten thousand by 10,000.

## CHAPTER XXII.

But because overly lengthy speech overwhelms the understanding and creates more error, I will describe the entire completion of the work in a few words. It is conducted in this way: one takes the Stone, which is well enough known, and through constant, steady labor, always perfectly sublimes <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified solid deposit</term> it, so that the Stone first becomes purified and subtle, until it reaches the highest subtlety and purity, and finally becomes volatile. And this is indeed the first degree of its governance.

Afterward, however, it must be made fixed <note>original: "fix"</note> according to the manner of fixation <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable in fire so that it no longer evaporates</term>, until it can remain at rest even in a strong fire.

The work for Silver should be very white, but the work for Gold should be very red.

For the White is a work of the Winter and the Red is a work of the Summer: therefore, it requires a further sublimation of its parts, according to its own manner, ripened with great decoction <note>A process of boiling or heating to extract the essence of a substance</note>, until it assumes an exceedingly red color. And in this alone consists the goal of the perfection of the second degree <note>original: "Gradus"</note>.

But in the third degree is the fulfillment of the entire work, and it is this: namely, that you make the now-fixed Stone volatile again by means of the non-fixed, according to the manner of sublimation, and the volatile again

It emphasizes that the Stone must be purified until it can withstand strong fire without evaporating.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Sublimation, Fixation, Rubedo, Alchemical Degrees, Gold, Silver</keywords>

fixed, and dissolve <note>original: "solvirest"</note> the fixed once more and make it volatile <note>original: "flüchtig"</note>, and again make the volatile fixed, until it flows and melts, and can certainly transform into permanent gold and silver.
    From the repeated preparation of this third degree arises the excellence of the Medicine <note>The "Medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the Universal Elixir</note>, and the increase of the transformation, so that it may infinitely transform each of the imperfect bodies <note>Base metals like lead or tin</note> into true gold and silver.
    Furthermore, above all other medicines, it has the powerful virtue to cure all diseases: Inasmuch as it gladdens the spirit <note>original: "Gemüthe"</note>, increases strength, preserves youth, and renews old age, since it does not allow the blood to become putrid, nor the <term>phlegm</term> (viscous mucus) to dominate, nor the bile to burn, nor the black bile <note>melancholy</note> to take the upper hand: Indeed, it greatly increases the life-blood that sits in the air passages, cleanses them, and preserves all members of the body, and in short, it cures both hot and cold weaknesses <note>Referring to the Galenic theory of humors where illnesses were classified by temperature</note> above all other medicines.
    For if the disease has lasted a month, it heals the same in one day: If it has lasted for a year, it heals in twelve days: But if it is older and has lasted a long time, it cures the same in one month, drives out all evil humors <note>original: "Feuchtigkeiten," referring to the bodily fluids</note>, and introduces good <term>humors</term> <note>The four vital fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note>: It also causes the person to whom it is given to be loved; it also gives undaunted courage, boldness, and victory to those who carry it with them.
    Thus is completed the greatest secret of all secrets of nature, which is the most precious treasure above all treasures of this world.

<summary>This page describes the final stabilization of the Philosopher's Stone through repeated fixation and dissolution, detailing its miraculous powers to transmute metals and provide universal healing and emotional strength.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, Philosopher's Stone, universal medicine, four humors, longevity, alchemy, gold and silver, healing</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the 'Vision' of John Dastin, a 14th-century English alchemist. It follows the standard alchemical trope of a dream or vision to explain the transmutation of metals.</meta>

# The Vision of the same John Dastin,
->Englishman.<-

Because all things that exist in nature have a certain effect of birth and multiplication, we take from their root a necessary significance of the planets <note>In alchemy, the seven planets correspond to the seven metals: Sun/Gold, Moon/Silver, Saturn/Lead, Jupiter/Tin, Mars/Iron, Venus/Copper, and Mercury/Quicksilver</note>. Therefore, as we sought with great desire the origin of gold and silver, we saw such a vision.

It seemed to us as if we were carried away before the face of Nature; and behold, seven planets came at the command of Nature, and made their firstborn brother king over them. And when they had placed the crown of the kingdom upon him, they worshipped him and made him understand their defects and infirmities: for they were almost all poisoned either with leprosy or with scabs and itch <note>Alchemists used medical terms like 'leprosy' to describe the impurities or oxidation found in 'base' metals compared to the 'perfection' of gold</note>.

The King, however, wished to comfort his fellow brothers and spoke: "It is certainly very good that one of you, who is without blemish, should die for all the others, so that the whole race does not perish, and that we, being anointed with his fresh blood, may attain health."

For, as Plato says, forms are given according to the merit of the materials <note>original: "secundum meritum materiarum dantur formae" - a common scholastic principle suggesting that the quality of the physical matter determines the soul or 'form' it can receive</note>.

But the other planets, when they had heard their King, spoke: "Who among us is without blemish? For we were conceived from impure seed, and our mother..."

born to us, so that we are subjected to frailty: Who can now make that pure, which is conceived from impure seed? For indeed, nothing is found in a thing that is not already within it.

To these, their mother, Mercury, answered: Dear sons, I have fathered six bodies, among which one is shining, unblemished, a king and head, and the best among the planets <note>In alchemy, the "planets" refer to the seven metals; the "king" here is Gold.</note>; there is nothing superfluous in him, nor too little, for his temperament is tempered, and his nature is balanced in heat and cold, in moisture and dryness.

Therefore, burning things do not burn him, nor do corrupting things corrupt him; neither earth nor water can ever change him. For this reason, the spirits are mixed with him, and are made fixed <note>term: fixed (fix gemacht) — in alchemy, this refers to making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat.</note> through him with great understanding, which is not to be found with a stubborn artist <note>term: artist (Künstler) — a common term for an alchemist or practitioner of the Hermetic arts.</note>.

Thus, we could all indeed become perfect together through him, but because he is perfect in his nature, he will surely not die. At this, they all wept violently and said: Oh, alas! Mother, why did you father us so impurely, but him so perfectly? Woe unto us! Why have you made us brothers of misery and heartache? Why were we not suffocated in the mother's womb, or otherwise perished before we were born? Why were we taken upon the lap and suckled at the breasts when we were first born, only that we should burn and be food for the fire?

To these, Nature answered thus: All things are created with wisdom and right measure; he who has given the counsel shall also provide the help. For a good prince lays down his life for his subjects. Therefore, you, O King, must be born anew, because otherwise you cannot die, nor can you make your brothers perfect and cure them. For he who converts the fourth into the sixth can transform it into the seventh, and back into the fourth. <note>This numerical sequence likely refers to the stages of elemental or planetary transformation in the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>

The King, however, smiled and spoke: How can an old body be born anew? Can he perhaps enter into his mother’s womb and be born again? <note>A direct allusion to the biblical dialogue between Jesus and Nicodemus in John 3:4, here applied to the "rebirth" of metals.</note>

Nature answered him: Are you the head of your race and do not know this? Do you not know that in Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note> nothing perishes but the Gold? When you are placed within your mother and her heart, there is nothing better than you: For in such a manner you can ensure that her volatile nature must rest and be retained within your substance. <term>Volatile: In alchemy, a substance that easily evaporates or escapes; "fixing" the volatile was a primary goal.</term>

For heavy things cannot be raised unless light things are added to them: Likewise, light things cannot be brought down without the company of heavy things: Thus, nothing is created without a cause.

Whoever loves his soul will lose it; for therein it appears as if he had lost what he was, and there he begins to appear as what he was not before. For that which destroys also improves, and from whence corruption comes, from there also arises improvement. <note>This reflects the alchemical principle of "putrefaction," where matter must appear to die or rot before it can be purified into a higher state.</note>

<summary>Nature instructs the King on the necessity of alchemical rebirth and the union of opposites (heavy and light, volatile and fixed) to achieve perfection. The text uses biblical metaphors to explain the chemical process of dissolving gold in mercury to reach a new state of matter.</summary>

Then the King sighed and spoke: Dear Governess, I pray that this burden be taken from me; yet, if it cannot be taken from me unless I bear it, then I will gladly be obedient to your will and desire. Now as the brothers rejoiced over the promised marriage of their King, they prepared for him a large chamber, a round bed, clear and bright, surrounded with dew and well-secured; nine virgins, beautiful of face, guarded this for the service of so great a King, and waited with fear for his deliverance.

Therefore, when all was now silent, a bright-shining light appeared in the midst, coming from the Royal Throne, and spoke to the nine virgins: I must pass through fire and water, yet I fear the serpent's poison, lest it might kill me with its exceeding great power. This happened on the first day of the <term>month of wine</term> <note>October; traditionally associated with the harvest and the beginning of the transformative winter months</note>. The beginning of the <term>Art</term> <note>The "Great Work" of alchemy</note> is the black <term>Raven’s Head</term> <note>original: "Rabenhaupt" – refers to Nigredo, the first stage of decomposition and purification</note>, which is dried little by little over the fire until, after the blackness, it finally puts on a white garment <note>Albedo, the second major stage of the work</note>.

Meanwhile, the time came for the woman to give birth, and she brought forth her first son. When she found none, she returned again to her little nest from which she had departed. Because she found no rest where she sought it, she spread her wings without ceasing and flew upward; but because the exit was closed, she was forced to return. And while she was struggling in this manner to fly away, our newborn son swallowed three maidens, so that their flight was entirely taken from them because their wings became featherless.

When the other six maidens saw this, they were greatly afraid, and with caution they moved very gently into the air. He, however, dried himself in the sun and was transformed from the black color into a white one. This occurred on the 28th of February. ## ALBERTUS MAGNUS
# COMPENDIUM.

->Or<-

### A Brief Concept of the Origin and
### Matter of Metals.

The first and most principal <term>Matter</term> <note>original: "Materia"</note> of all metals in their <term>Ores</term> <note>original: "Mineris" or "Erzgruben"</note> from which they have their origin, is a dry water, which we call the living water or <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum," commonly known as mercury</note>: And a stinking <term>Spirit</term>, which we otherwise call <term>Sulphur</term>: But yet not in their common nature as they are created in their mines; for in the places where they are generated, no finished metal is found. Rather, this same matter is a substance created by nature which possesses the properties and essence of those two aforementioned: And from such matter or substance, which comes from the two previously mentioned, there is produced and created a subtle essence, like a smoke in the veins of the earth and mineral passages, where they gather and are sustained.

<summary>This page introduces the alchemical theory of Albertus Magnus regarding the "Mercury-Sulphur" composition of metals, describing their origin as a subtle vapor within the earth's veins.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, alchemy, metallurgy, quicksilver, sulphur, mineral generation, matter of metals</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the initial stages of metallic formation; this page continues the explanation of how natural heat acts upon matter to create a permanent union of elements.</meta>

-> ) o ( <-

And thereafter, when the natural force overcomes the unnatural matter, it renders the same diminished and hard, and unites itself with it in a firm, inseparable union through the natural cooking <note>The term "cooking" or "coction" refers to the alchemical belief that metals "ripen" in the earth through steady, internal heat.</note>.

From this it is to be seen that the four elements are present in the metals in a natural way, and that they are of the same essence, since they are nothing other than very subtle vapors which, through natural cooking in their mines or veins, have coagulated, become fixed, and been converted into metallic substance.

Likewise, it is to be understood from this that the fundamental moisture of the metals cannot be consumed or separated from them during their calcination <note>The process of reducing a metal to powder through heat</note> because of their uniformity and the strong union of the elements with one another—unlike how the moisture of a stone is separated—for in metals, the moisture is united with a great dryness.

## The Way and Manner of Nature in the Birthing of Metals is Very Wonderful

There is in the mines a uniform matter, namely **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argentum vivum"</note> and Sulfur in one body, from which matter Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy as a refinement of natural processes.</note> easily and naturally produces a vaporous essence or subtle matter, which is converted into a metal through the help of Nature. This vaporous matter is a body, and the steam generated from it is

<summary>This page describes the alchemical formation of metals through the "cooking" of vapors and the inseparable union of sulfur and mercury within the earth. It emphasizes that metallic moisture is unique because it cannot be easily destroyed by heat due to its perfect internal balance.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, metallic generation, coction, calcination, quicksilver, sulfur, four elements, mineral veins</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical transmutation and the relationship between spirit and matter, likely following a description of the initial preparation of the "Stone" or the "First Matter."</meta>

->* ) 0 ( *<-

is a <term>Spirit</term> <note>original: "Spiritus"; in alchemy, this refers to a volatile substance or the vital essence of a material</note>. Thus Nature makes a <term>Spirit</term> out of the body: and so makes it ascend from the Earth into the Heaven, that is, she makes the corporeal thing spiritual, and thereafter through <term>Circulation</term> <note>the process of continuous distillation and condensation in a closed vessel</note> she makes the spiritual thing bodily again, so that it thus descends from the Heaven back into the Earth.

It is also to be seen from this that all metals, as concerns the root of Nature, are of one and the same <term>Substance</term> or <term>Matter</term>: and yet they are not of the same figure or form. And this happens because of the weakness and strength, purity and impurity, scarcity or abundance of the <term>Substance</term> of <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Queckſilber"</note> and <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original: "Schweffel"</note> in their natural union and composition: and because of the differing properties of the <term>Ores</term> <note>original: "Mineren"</note>, and also Nature’s own longer or shorter <term>Ripening</term> <note>original: "Zeitigung"</note> or <term>Digestion</term> <note>original: "Decoction"; the process of heating a substance in a fluid to extract its essence</note>.

The <term>Quick-silver</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum"</note> and the <term>Sulphurs</term> have a particular <term>Matter</term> or <term>Substance</term> which is vaporous and very subtle, brought about by our Art from the nature of the two previously mentioned; it is very bright and clear as an eye-tear, in which the spirit of the <term>Fifth Essence</term> <note>original: "fünften Weſens" or "Quinta Essentia"; the purest, most medicinal element beyond the four earthly elements</note> dwells and is hidden, as we shall mention below: yet that same <term>Substance</term> is not that <term>Sulphur</term>, nor the <term>Quick-silver</term> as they are by nature in their <term>Ores</term>, but is only a part of those two, which is neither <term>Sulphur</term> nor <term>Quick-silver</term>.

Some <term>Philosophers</term> <note>referring to the "Philosophers by Fire" or Alchemists</note> say that from the <term>Quick-silver</term> alone, without the mixing of the <term>Sulphurs</term>—

how this **Matter** is generated: It is, however, exactly the same, for **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argentum vivum"</note> naturally contains within itself a red **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> mixed with it; and this red **Sulfur** we have seen, and with our own hand separated it from its whiteness.

The aforementioned vaporous **Substance** can by no means be naturally drawn from anywhere else, except solely from the **Substance** of the two aforementioned things, namely from the **Quicksilver** and Sulfur which are thus mixed together.

The Earth and the Air are passive **Elements**: Water and Fire are active. Water is the husband, and Earth is the wife.

Likewise, you should note that in our causing the aforementioned vaporous **Matter** to be generated from the **Substances** of the two aforementioned—namely **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurii"</note> and **Sulfur** and their related parts—that the stated **Substance** is at the same time to some extent united with the **Body** <note>original: "Cörper"</note> from which the misty **Substance** arises or comes forth.

For the artist has caused a bodily **Matter** or **Substance** to rise from the Earth into Heaven, and become a spiritual **Matter** or essence. And when afterwards this same **Matter** or **Substance** is made **spiritual**, it coagulates, becomes hard, and is turned into a stone: Thus he makes it descend again from Heaven to the Earth: And the **Spiritual Matter** or **Substance** he makes **corporeal** <note>original: "corporalisch"</note> once again.

) 0 (
D 2

And a **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophus"; likely referring to an ancient authority like Hermes Trismegistus or Geber</note> says that from the **Substance of Mercury** alone, without the addition of the **Substance of Sulfur**, the Stone can be extracted and brought forth. He does not understand this to mean the previously mentioned vaporous substance in which the Spirit of the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: "Quinta Essentia"; the purest, most concentrated medicinal or alchemical force</note> is hidden—which is what we seek and with which we can **tincture** <note>original: "tingiren"; to transform or dye base metals into gold</note>—but rather he speaks of the fixed and constant substance of the Stone. This is its "Earth," in which that same **Spirit** is **fixed** <note>original: "figiret"; rendered non-volatile or solid</note>. Through this Earth, the **spiritual** Stone is conceived in the "belly of the wind" <note>A reference to the Emerald Tablet of Hermes: "The wind carried it in its belly."</note> through the mastery of our art.

When the **Philosophers** observe their **Matter** as it sits in its vessel and feels a certain warmth, a vapor rises from it in the form of a smoke and ascends into the upper part of the vessel; this they call a **Sublimation**. And when they saw that it came down again to the bottom of the vessel, they called it a **Distillation**. When they further saw that the **Matter** became thick and black and gave off an evil odor, they called it a **Putrefaction** <note>The stage of decay or breaking down</note> and the **Raven’s Head** <note>original: "Rabenhaupt"; the alchemical symbol for the black stage, or nigredo</note>.

When they saw that after a long time the **Matter** lost its black, dark, and evil color and reached an ash-gray color, and to some extent a whiteness, they called it **incineration** <note>reduction to ashes</note> or **whitening** <note>original: "dealbation"; the albedo stage of the Great Work</note>.

Therefore, the **Sum** or content of the entire art is nothing other than an extraction of the water from the earth, and a pouring down of the water back onto the earth, until the earth becomes putrefied.

<summary>This page explains that the Alchemical Stone is formed by fixing a spirit within 'earth' and describes the visual stages of the process, including sublimation, the black 'Raven's Head' stage, and the subsequent whitening.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Sulfur, Fifth Essence, Sublimation, Putrefaction, Raven's Head, Albedo, Alchemy, Distillation</keywords>

be. And this earth putrefies <note>The stage of 'putrefaction' or decay, essential for rebirth in alchemy.</note> with the water, and when it shall be purified through the help of Him who governs all, then the entire masterpiece shall be completed.

Behold! The sperm or seed of the philosophers <note>original: "Sperma oder Samen der Philoſophorum"</note> is a living water; the earth, however, is an imperfect body <note>original: "Corpus"</note>: which earth is called a mother of all elements.

The solution <note>dissolving</note> or dissolution of the body, and the coagulation <note>thickening</note> or hardening are indeed two distinct things, but they involve only one labor; for the body is not dissolved without the spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> also being simultaneously coagulated.

When the water is conjoined with the earth, the water undertakes to dissolve the earth with its moisture and power; for it makes the earth more subtle than it was before: and makes the earth like unto itself, because the water is thinner and more subtle than the earth. The soul does the same within the body, and in such a manner the water is made thick with the earth.

Therefore, there is no distinction between the solution of the body and the coagulation of the spirit as far as time is concerned, nor is there perhaps a different task or other labor.

When the earth is dissolved into a black powder, and begins to retain a little of the mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note> within itself, then the male works into the female—that is, Azoth <note>A universal solvent or the essential agent of transformation in alchemy</note> works into the earth: for as long as the water holds the dominion, the earth is multiplied and increased.

# An Epistle
## OF JOHANNES PONTANI,

I, Johannes Pontanus, have traveled through many lands so that I might learn something certain regarding the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life</note>, and though I have wandered through nearly the entire world, I have encountered many false deceivers and no true philosophers. However, I have always studied diligently, and after manifold doubts, I have finally come upon the path of truth.

But after the matter became somewhat known to me, I nevertheless erred many times before I discovered the right material, its effects, and how to work with it.

Initially, I set to work with the matter in such a way that I <term>putrefied</term> <note>The process of breaking down or "rotting" a substance to its base components</note> it for nine months, but found nothing. I also kept it in a <term>Bain-marie</term> <note>original: "Balneo Mar."; a heated water bath used for gentle, uniform heating</note> for a period of time, and likewise erred. After that, I kept it in a <term>calcination fire</term> <note>A high-heat furnace used to reduce substances to ash or powder</note> for three months, and that was also not correctly managed. I have undertaken and attempted all types of distillation and sublimation (as the philosophers speak, or seem to speak, such as <term>Geber</term> <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational 8th-century alchemist</note>, <term>Archelaus</term> <note>A 5th-century BCE Greek philosopher often cited in alchemical traditions</note>, and almost all others); but I found nothing. Finally, I [tried] the <term>subject</term> <note>The primary raw material or "prime matter" of the alchemical work</note> of the entire alchemical art in every way that one could imagine, and which are performed by means of dung, the water bath, and ashes

<summary>Johannes Pontanus recounts his extensive travels and numerous failed laboratory experiments—including putrefaction and various heating methods—in his long search for the true secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Pontanus, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, laboratory methods, Geber, Archelaus, calcination, distillation</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---

# ❦ ( 0 ) ❧

and other fires of many kinds, which are nonetheless found in the books of the philosophers, I have attempted to prepare, but I have found nothing of value.

Therefore, I have diligently read the books of the philosophers for three years together, especially *Hermes* <note>original: "Hermetem"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> alone, whose brief words contain within them the entire science of the Stone; although he speaks in dark riddles concerning the Upper and the Lower, and the Heaven and the Earth.

Thus, our instrument, in which the matter is brought into its true being during the first, second, and third operations, is not a fire of the *Bath* <note>original: "Balnei"; referring to the Bain-marie or water bath</note>, nor of horse-manure, nor of ashes, nor of any other kind that the wise men have set down in their books.

What kind of fire is it then, that performs the entire work from beginning to end? It is certain that the philosophers have kept the same secret and concealed.

But I, moved by sincere love, wish to explain this to you, as well as the preparation of the entire work.

Thus, our Stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the goal of the alchemical Great Work</note> is but one thing, to which many names are given; therefore it will be difficult for you before you learn to recognize it.

For it is of a watery, airy, fiery, and earthy nature: it is phlegmatic, choleric, and melancholic <note>The author is linking the Stone to the four humors of ancient medicine</note>: it is Sulfur and at the same time also Mercury, and has many superfluities which—as surely as GOD lives—are converted into a right and true essence by means of our fire. And whoever [knows] <insert>anything</insert> of the *Subject* <note>original: "Subjecto"; the base material or starting point of the alchemical process</note>...

<column-break/>

->something<-

<summary>The author rejects common heating methods like water baths or manure, claiming that a secret "fire" is required to transform the multifaceted "Stone" into its true essence. He promises to reveal this secret, which he discovered after three years of studying the cryptic writings of Hermes Trismegistus.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Philosopher's Stone, Alchemical Fire, Four Humors, Sulfur and Mercury, Alchemical Instruments, Secret Knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the alchemical fire and the transformation of the "Subject" or base material. It emphasizes that nothing is discarded in this specific process, contrary to common chemical practices.</meta>

<header>❦ ) o ( ❦ 219</header>

Whoever separates or demands anything be removed, in the opinion
that such a thing is necessary, truly understands nothing
in <term>Philosophy</term><note>Here meaning Alchemy, the "Natural Philosophy."</note>; For the superfluous,
the impure, the ugly, the cloudy—indeed, ultimately the en-
tire substance of the <term>Subject</term><note>The starting material or "Prima Materia" used in the work.</note>—is brought by means of
our fire into a fixed, spiritual body
and made perfect.

And this the Sages have never openly dis-
closed; therefore also few have been able to attain the <term>Art</term><note>The Great Work of alchemy.</note>,
because they imagine that such super-
fluity and impurity must be present and cast aside.

Now we must seek out the properties of our
fire, and whether it is suited to our
material in such a way as I have mentioned:
namely, that the material is changed, all the
while this same fire does not burn the material,
separates nothing from the material, and does not divide the pure from
the impure (as all the <term>Philosophers</term><note>The ancient alchemical authorities.</note> say),
but rather it converts the entire subject into
a pure being. It does not sublimate according to the
manner in which <term>Geber</term><note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a famous 8th-century alchemist known for complex laboratory procedures.</note> performs his <term>sublimations</term><note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collects as a solid.</note>,
likewise also <term>Arnoldus</term><note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova, a 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> and others, when they
speak of sublimation and distillation, saying that
these are performed in a short time.

It is mineral-like, uniform, and persistent:
It does not give off steam or vapor, unless
it is over-excited. It has something of
<term>Sulfur</term><note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle.</note> about it. It is not taken from any
material, but from elsewhere. It
breaks everything apart; it dissolves and makes hard;
likewise, it makes dense and <term>calcinates</term><note>Reduces a substance to a powder through heat.</note>, and is
to be discovered through artifice; it is a great advantage
obtained without any, or even the slightest, expense.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>The author argues that true alchemy does not discard "impurities" but transforms the entire substance into a perfected spiritual body using a unique, inexpensive "fire." He contrasts this internal transformation with the traditional laboratory methods of Geber and Arnoldus de Villa Nova.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Subject, spiritual body, Alchemical Fire, Geber, Arnoldus de Villa Nova, Sublimation, Sulfur, Calcination, transmutation</keywords>

And that same fire is to be maintained with a moderate under-firing, for the entire work is accomplished with a gentle heat, which simultaneously performs all the <term>sublimations</term><note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the material.</note> that belong to it.

Those who read Geber <note>original: "Gebrum"; referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational 8th-century alchemist.</note> and all the other philosophers, even if they lived for a hundred thousand years, they would still not comprehend it; for this fire is discovered solely through deep reflection, and only then—and not before—can it be understood in their books.

Therefore, the error in this Art is this: that one cannot discover the fire which makes and transforms the entire matter into a true <term>Philosopher's Stone</term><note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>.

For that reason, apply yourself diligently to this; for if I had found this fire in the beginning, I would not have erred so many times in the <term>practice</term><note>The physical laboratory work as opposed to theoretical study.</note> of the matter.

Thus, it is no wonder to me that so many and such great people have not arrived at the completion of the work.

They err, they have erred, and they will continue to err, because the philosophers have not set down the actual "agent" <note>The active principle or specific type of heat required for the transformation.</note>, except for one, who is called Artephius; yet even he speaks for himself. And if I had not read Artephius and noted his speeches, I would never have come to the completion of the work.

The preparation of the work is this: One should take the stone and grind it as diligently as possible according to the natural way, and then let it come to the fire; but one must [observe] the proportion

<summary>The author emphasizes that the secret to the Great Work lies in understanding a specific "gentle fire" discovered through meditation rather than literal reading, citing the alchemist Artephius as his primary inspiration. portion or equality of the fire, namely that the <term>Matter</term><note>original: "Materia"; the physical substance being transformed in the alchemical vessel</note> be only encouraged or awakened; then that same fire will, in a short time and without any manual labor, certainly complete the entire work. For it will <term>putrefy</term><note>original: "putreficiren"; the stage of breaking down or "death" of the substance</note>, destroy, regenerate, and bring to perfection. It will cause the three principal colors to appear, namely **Black**, **White**, and **Red**. <note>These colors represent the three main stages of the alchemical process: Nigredo (blackness/decay), Albedo (whiteness/purification), and Rubedo (redness/perfection).</note>

Accordingly, you should strive with all your might to know and explore your fire, and you will surely attain it; for it performs the entire work and is the <term>Key of all the Sages</term><note>original: "Schlüssel aller Weisen"</note>, which they have never fully disclosed or revealed.

However, if you will reflect well and deeply upon the aforementioned words regarding the property of the fire, it will become known to you, and not otherwise.

I have written this moved by love and loyalty; but so that I may satisfy you, you should know that the fire is not changed with the Matter, for it is not of the Matter, as I mentioned above.

I have wished to indicate this and remind the intelligent so that they do not spend their money uselessly, but may instead know what they ought to seek: In such a manner, and no other, will they arrive at the truth of the <term>Art</term><note>original: "Kunst"; refers to Alchemy, the "Sacred Art"</note>. Be commended to **GOD**.

## The Whole Art Briefly Composed.

I advise you to take that which Nature has given,
and put it in a glass; seal it quite evenly.
Moisten it with moist fire <note>original: "feuchtem Feur"; likely referring to a water bath or "balneum Mariae" used for gentle, steady heating</note>, as the
->Sages teach you,<-
so it will finally become that which your heart
->desires.<-

## On the Power and Effect of the Prepared Stone.

->Master. Student.<-

Master: I take this.
Student: Oh, dear [Master], what?
Master: A green grass <note>original: "grünes Graß"; a common alchemical metaphor for the "Green Lion" or the raw, living mineral matter at the start of the Great Work</note>,
Put it in a vessel <note>original: "Baß"; likely a phonetic or archaic rendering of "Fass" (vat/vessel) or "Basis"</note>,
And make it wet,
So it becomes that.
Student: Do tell me! What?
Master: A noble treasure,
That keeps body and blood
Healthy.
In this world
Nothing is its equal,

It makes one rich,
For the metals,
Lead, Copper, Steel,
Are into Gold converted.
Whatever one desires,
That can one have,
Through these gifts.

Preserves youth,
The gray hairs
Fall out entirely; <note>The text suggests that gray hair is replaced by the hair of one's youth.</note>
Sickness it drives away,
Before it cannot remain
The Gout <note>original: "Podagra"</note>,
Epilepsy <note>original: "Epilepsia"</note>:
Leprosy, Quartan fever <note>A form of Malaria where fever strikes every fourth day.</note>

It helps with the ache <note>original: "Gicht" - likely referring to joint pain or rheumatism.</note>,
The stone <note>Kidney or bladder stones.</note> it breaks.
Yes, its power
And property
Abundantly gives
What a person loves:
Brings joyfulness,
Drives away sorrow,
Makes it so that a glass
May be hammered, <note>A reference to "vitrum flexile" or malleable glass, a legendary alchemical discovery.</note>
Yet does not break.
Strengthens the sight,
Sharpens the hearing,
I say no more,
To **GOD** be praise, prize, and honor!

<summary>A rhyming German poem detailing the miraculous medical and transformative powers of the Philosopher's Stone, ranging from curing gout to creating malleable glass.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Transmutation, Universal Medicine, Longevity, Gold, Healing, Malleable Glass, Divine Praise</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous discourse on the difficulty of alchemical study, shifting from technical frustration to a spiritual and natural philosophy.</meta>

( * ) 0 ( * )

You complain that the Art is not clearly described,
And how one might go the straightest way to the Work;
Take this for an answer: In no book does one find
How to begin so that one might conceive a child.
And yet the work is so very easily accomplished,
That even a peasant lad, who has never seen it,
Can perform the same. Yes, indeed. But you say:
More belongs to the dance than a pair of new
-> shoes / <-
There must also be a pair of straight legs.
The floor must be level, not full of sharp stones:
But here I do not find what one should rightly take
For the beginning of this Art, so that it might turn out well.
The Philosophers <note>original: "Philoſophi"; referring here to the ancient alchemists and sages</note> also often lay down great stones
With unknown words, which then move a person
And lead them now here, now onto another path,
So that a thorough understanding can nowhere be grasped.
To this, have this report: The Sages all write
That God reveals this to him who pleases Him well;
You must call upon Him, that He may enlighten
Your understanding, otherwise the Work remains unknown to you.
Also, the Philosophers are by no means to be hated,
For they have left behind good accounts in their writings,
And as much as God allowed them to show
Of this masterpiece <note>original: "Meiſterſtück"; the Great Work of alchemy</note>, that they have done.
Therefore, if you wish for the Art to succeed for you,
Then pray to God, that He might bring you onto the right path:
And follow Nature, and also consider her possibilities
Rightly therein. Herewith you have your answer.

-> Farewell. <note>original: "Vale"</note> <-

-> H. R. C. <note>Likely the initials of the author or a Rosicrucian attribution</note> <-
-> T H E  E N D. <-
